\mathrm{vd}(\{I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\})=0\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\sigma_{I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}}}\frac{\widehat{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(R\Gamma(X,F\otimes L)\otimes y^{-1})}{\sqrt{(-1)^{\frac{\mathrm{vd}}{2}}\widehat{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}[3])}}\in\mathbb{Q}(t^{\frac{1}{2}},y^{\frac{1}{2}}).

Similarly, we define
equivariant tautological invariants

\displaystyle\langle L\rangle_{X,v}^{\mathrm{PT}_{q}}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}P\in\curly P^{T}\\
\mathrm{vd}(\{P\})=0\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\sigma_{P}}\frac{e((R\pi_{\curly P*}(\mathbb{F}\otimes L))|_{P}^{\vee}\otimes y)}{\sqrt{(-1)^{\frac{\mathrm{vd}}{2}}e(T_{\curly P}^{\mathrm{vir}}|_{P}})}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}=[\mathcal{O}_{X}\to F]\in\curly P^{T}\\
\mathrm{vd}(\{I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\})=0\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\sigma_{I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}}}\frac{e(R\Gamma(X,F\otimes L)^{\vee}\otimes y)}{\sqrt{(-1)^{\frac{\mathrm{vd}}{2}}e(R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}[1])}},

which is an element of
A_{*}^{T}(\mathrm{pt})_{\mathrm{loc}}(m)=\mathbb{Q}(s,m)
.

Although the above invariants are liberally defined, we of course expect that geometrically meaningful choices of square roots are induced by a global orientation. We then conjecture the following equivariant
K
-theoretic
\mathrm{DT}
–
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
correspondence:

Conjecture 3.14
.

Let
X
be a Calabi-Yau 4-fold with action by an algebraic torus
T
preserving the Calabi-Yau volume form. Let
L
be a
T
-equivariant line bundle on
X
. Let
\gamma\in H^{4}_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q})
,
\beta\in H^{6}_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q})
, and suppose
\curly P_{\gamma,\beta,n}^{(q)}(X)^{T}
consists of isolated reduced points for all
n
and
q\in\{-1,0\}
. Then there are choices of square roots associated to all
T
-fixed points such that

\frac{\sum_{n}\langle\!\langle L\rangle\!\rangle_{X,\gamma,\beta,n}^{\mathrm{DT}}q^{n}}{\sum_{n}\langle\!\langle L\rangle\!\rangle_{X,0,0,n}^{\mathrm{DT}}q^{n}}=\sum_{n}\langle\!\langle L\rangle\!\rangle_{X,\gamma,\beta,n}^{\mathrm{PT}_{0}}q^{n}

as an identity of Laurent series in
q
with coefficients in
\mathbb{Q}(t^{\frac{1}{2}},y^{\frac{1}{2}})
.

4.
Virtual Lefschetz principle

In this section, we present two virtual Lefschetz principles for a smooth connected 3-fold
Y
in a Calabi-Yau 4-fold
X
: one in the case
X
is projective, and one in the case
X=\mathrm{Tot}(K_{Y})
and
Y
is toric.

4.1.
Via virtual pull-back

For any smooth projective variety
X
and smooth connected effective divisor
\iota:Y\hookrightarrow X
, we can push-forward a
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pair
(F,s)
on
Y
to a
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pair on
X
as follows

\mathcal{O}_{X}\to\iota_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota_{*}s}}{{\to}}\iota_{*}F.

Fixing a Chern character
v\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})
, we use the notation

\curly P_{v}^{(q)}(Y):=\bigsqcup_{\iota_{*}v^{\prime}=v\cdot\mathrm{td}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(Y))}\curly P_{v^{\prime}}^{(q)}(Y),

where the union is over all
v^{\prime}\in H^{*}(Y,\mathbb{Q})
satisfying
\iota_{*}v^{\prime}=v\cdot\mathrm{td}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(Y))
.
Then we have a closed embedding

\imath=\iota_{*}:\curly P_{v}^{(q)}(Y)\hookrightarrow\curly P_{v}^{(q)}(X),

where the Chern characters match by Grothendieck-Riemann-Roch. We denote the universal pairs of
\curly P_{v}^{(q)}(Y)
and
\curly P_{\iota_{*}v}^{(q)}(X)
by
\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y}=[\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{F}_{Y}]
and
\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}=[\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{F}]
respectively.
The main result of this section is the following virtual Lefschetz principle.

Theorem 4.1
.

Let
X
be a projective Calabi-Yau 4-fold and
\iota:Y\hookrightarrow X
a smooth connected effective divisor. Let
q\in\{-1,0,1\}
and
v\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})
. Let
\curly P_{Y}:=\curly P_{v}^{(q)}(Y)
,
\curly P_{X}:=\curly P_{v}^{(q)}(X)
, and
L:=\mathcal{O}_{X}(Y)
. Assume the following:

\mathrm{A1)}

The tautological complex
{\curly L}:=R\pi_{\curly P_{X}*}(\mathbb{F}\otimes L)
is a vector bundle concentrated in degree 0.

\mathrm{A2)}

The canonical map

\phi_{Y}:\mathbb{E}_{Y}:=R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi_{\curly P_{Y}}}(\mathbb{I}_{Y}^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},\mathbb{I}_{Y}^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}\otimes K_{Y})_{0}[2]\to\mathbb{L}_{\curly P_{Y}}

induced by the Atiyah class of
\mathbb{I}_{Y}^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}
is a perfect, i.e. 2-term, obstruction theory on
\curly P_{Y}
.

\mathrm{A3)}

The moduli space
\curly P_{Y}
is connected.

Then we have

\imath_{*}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\curly P_{Y}}=\widehat{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(R\pi_{\curly P_{X}*}(\mathbb{F}\otimes L))^{\vee}\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\curly P_{X}},

where
\imath:\curly P_{Y}\hookrightarrow\curly P_{X}
is the inclusion map.

Remark 4.2
.

In the setting of the theorem, we also have the following cohomological virtual Lefschetz principle

\imath_{*}[\curly P_{Y}]^{\mathrm{vir}}=e(R\pi_{\curly P_{X}*}(\mathbb{F}\otimes L))\cap[\curly P_{X}]^{\mathrm{vir}}.

We discuss the assumptions in the theorem.

•

For
q=-1,0
and
\gamma=0
, condition A2) is automatic and
\curly P_{Y}
are the familiar
\mathrm{DT},\mathrm{PT}
spaces. In this case, Theorem
4.1
was proved by the third-named author
[
Par
]
.

•

For
q=-1
and
\gamma=\beta=0
, conditions A1), A2), A3) are all satisfied (Hilbert schemes of points on a connected quasi-projective scheme are connected).
In this case Theorem
4.1
was proved by the third-named author
[
Par
]
, which establishes a conjecture of the second-named author and Cao
[
CK1
]
.

•

Suppose for all closed points
(F,s)
of
\curly P_{X}
, we have
H^{>0}(X,F\otimes L)=0
. Then A1) is satisfied by cohomology and base change.

•

The assumption A2) can be divided into two conditions:
1.
\phi_{Y}
is an obstruction theory,
and 2.
\mathbb{E}_{Y}
is a
2
-term perfect complex (i.e. has tor-amplitude
[-1,0]
).
The first part is not automatic for
\gamma\neq 0
since the codimension of pairs in
Y
is
1\not\geq 2
.

•

We show below that if
H^{>0}(X,F\otimes L)=0
for all closed points
(F,s)
of
\curly P_{X}
and
Y
is Calabi-Yau, then A2) also follows.

•

Assumption A3) is a technical assumption related to orientations. If we drop assumption A3), then we obtain the following weaker conclusion: for each connected component
\curly P_{Y}^{i}
of
\curly P_{Y}
, there exists a sign
(-1)^{\sigma(i)}
determined by the choice of orientation on
\curly P_{X}
, such that

(14)

\sum_{i}(-1)^{\sigma(i)}\imath_{*}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\curly P^{i}_{Y}}=\widehat{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(R\pi_{\curly P_{X}*}(\mathbb{F}\otimes L))^{\vee}\otimes\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\curly P_{X}}.

We expect that for any choice of orientation on
\curly P_{X}
, the signs
(-1)^{\sigma(i)}
are independent of
i
.

Proof of Theorem
4.1
.

This is a variant of the proof of
[
Par
, Thm. 3.1]
. (See also
[
Par
, Cor. B.4]
for the
K
-theoretical statement.)
Here we give a sketch proof focussing on the differences with loc. cit.
We use the following notation.
Both the projections
\curly P_{Y}\times Y,\curly P_{Y}\times X\to\curly P_{Y}
are denoted by
\pi
.
We let

\mathbb{I}_{X}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}:=\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}|_{\curly P_{Y}\times X},\qquad\mathbb{E}_{X}:=\mathbb{E}|_{\curly P_{Y}}=R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{X},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{X})_{0}[3],

where
\mathbb{E}
was defined in (
7
). Also note that
d_{*}\mathbb{F}_{Y}\cong\mathbb{F}|_{\curly P_{Y}\times X}

for the inclusion
d:\curly P_{Y}\times Y\hookrightarrow\curly P_{Y}\times X
.

We first observe that
\curly P_{Y}
is the scheme-theoretical zero locus of the tautological section

\tau\in\Gamma(\curly P_{X},\curly L)
induced by
\mathcal{O}_{{\curly P_{X}}\times X}(-Y)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{{\curly P_{X}}\times X}\xrightarrow{s}\mathbb{F}

\textstyle{\curly L\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\curly P_{Y}\cong Z(\tau)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\imath}
\textstyle{\curly P_{X}.\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}

This is an analog of
[
Par
, Prop. 3.6]
.
The only difference is when we apply
[
Par
, Lem. 3.7]
, we use the following simple fact on a
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pair
(F,s)
on
X
:
if
G
is a
q
-dimensional sheaf on
X
, then
\operatorname{Hom}(G,F)=0
.
(An alternative proof is to use the derived moduli of pairs (cf.
[
BKP
, Rem. 3.9]
) based on
[
BKP
, Lem. 3.7]
. Then we can show that the relative cotangent complex for the canonical map
R\curly P_{Y}\to Z_{R\curly P_{X}}(R\tau)
is concentrated in degrees
\leq-2
by the argument in
[
Par
, Rem. 3.8]
and thus its classical truncation is an isomorphism.)

We then note that there exists a
3
-term symmetric obstruction theory

\overline{\phi}_{Y}:=\phi_{Y}\circ\epsilon:\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}\to\mathbb{E}_{Y}\to\mathbb{L}_{Y}

given by some
3
-term symmetric complex
\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}
and a self-dual exact triangle

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{Y}^{\vee}[2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon^{\vee}[2]}
\textstyle{\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}^{\vee}[2]\cong\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\epsilon}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{Y}}

as in
[
Par
, Prop. 3.10]
.
Indeed, we have a canonical self-dual exact triangle

\textstyle{R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y})[4]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(d_{*}\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y},d_{*}\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y})[3]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y}\otimes L)[2]}

given by the Serre duality for the divisor
d:\curly P_{Y}\times Y\hookrightarrow\curly P_{Y}\times X
(cf.
[
Par
, Lem. 3.11]
).
The desired
3
-term symmetric complex
\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}
can be obtained from the above triangle after removing the traces in the left and right terms.
This is the technical part but can be done by the arguments in
[
Par
, Lem. 3.12]
after checking that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{{\curly P}_{Y}\times X}(d_{*}\mathcal{O}_{{\curly P}_{Y}\times Y},R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{d}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y}\otimes L)[-2])

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{{\curly P}_{Y}\times Y}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y}\otimes L[-2])\oplus\operatorname{Hom}_{{\curly P}_{Y}\times Y}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y}\otimes L^{\vee},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y}\otimes L[-3])=0.

Consequently, the Behrend-Fantechi virtual cycle associated to the
2
-term perfect obstruction theory
\phi_{Y}
is equal to the Oh-Thomas virtual cycle associated to the
3
-term symmetric obstruction theory
\overline{\phi}_{Y}
(up to sign) by the reduction formula
[
Par
, Prop. 1.18]
(see
[
Par
, B.2]
for the
K
-theoretical version)

(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\curly P_{Y}})^{\mathrm{OT}}_{\overline{\phi}_{Y}}=(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\curly P_{Y}})^{\mathrm{BF}}_{\phi_{Y}}\in K_{0}(\curly P_{Y},\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2}]),

where
(\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\curly P_{Y}})^{\mathrm{BF}}_{\phi_{Y}}:=(\mathcal{O}_{\curly P_{Y}}^{\mathrm{vir}})^{\mathrm{BF}}_{\phi_{Y}}\cdot(\det(\mathbb{E}_{Y}))^{\frac{1}{2}}
is the twisted virtual structure sheaf.

We show that the two
3
-term symmetric complexes
\mathbb{E}_{X}
and
\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}

fit into a reduction diagram (in the sense of
[
Par
, Prop. 1.5]
)

\textstyle{\mathbb{D}^{\vee}[2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha^{\vee}[2]}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{\vee}[2]}
\textstyle{\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}^{\vee}[2]\cong\overline{\mathbb{E}}_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{\curly L|_{\curly P_{Y}}^{\vee}[1]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{X}^{\vee}[2]|_{\curly P_{Y}}\cong\mathbb{E}_{X}|_{\curly P_{Y}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{\mathbb{D}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\gamma}
\textstyle{\curly L|_{\curly P_{Y}}^{\vee}[1],}

for some perfect complex
\mathbb{D}
and maps
\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\delta
.
Indeed, it is straightforward to form a canonical diagram

\textstyle{R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(d_{*}\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{X})[3]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(d_{*}\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y},d_{*}\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y})[3]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(d_{*}\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y},\mathcal{O}_{\curly P_{Y}}\boxtimes L^{\vee})[4]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{X},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{X})_{0}[3]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{X},d_{*}\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y})[3]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(d_{*}\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y},\mathcal{O}_{\curly P_{Y}}\boxtimes L^{\vee})[4]}

where the rows are exact and the right square commutes as in
[
Par
, 3.14]
.
Again the technical part is removing some traces,
but this can be done by the arguments in
[
Par
, Prop. 3.13]
.
A necessary additional ingredient is:

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}_{\curly P_{Y}\times X}(d_{*}\mathbb{F}_{Y},d_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\curly P_{Y}\times Y})

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Hom}_{\curly P_{Y}\times Y}(\mathbb{F}_{Y},\mathcal{O}_{\curly P_{Y}\times Y})\oplus\operatorname{Hom}_{\curly P_{Y}\times Y}(\mathbb{F}_{Y},\mathcal{O}_{\curly P_{Y}\times Y}\otimes L[-1])=0.

This follows since the underlying sheaves of
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pairs on
Y
are still torsion.

Finally, the
3
-term symmetric obstructions theories
\phi
and
\overline{\phi}_{Y}
are compatible
and thus we can apply the virtual pullback formula
[
Par
, Thm. 2.2]
.
Indeed, we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(d_{*}\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{X})[3]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(d_{*}\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y},d_{*}\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y})[3]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{At}_{\curly P_{Y}\times X}(d_{*}\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y})}
\textstyle{R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y}\otimes L)[2]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{At}_{\curly P_{Y}\times Y}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{Y})}
\textstyle{R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{X},\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{X})[3]\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{At}_{\curly P_{X}\times X}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{At}_{\curly P_{Y}\times X}(\mathbb{I}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}_{X})}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{\curly P_{X}}|_{\curly P_{Y}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{\curly P_{Y}}}

by the basic properties of Atiyah classes (cf.
[
Par
, 3.4.5]
).
By removing the traces, we have the compatibility condition (in Definition
[
Par
, Def. 2.1]
) and thus we have the
K
-theoretic virtual pullback formula
[
Par
, Thm. B.3]

\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\curly P_{Y}}=\widehat{\imath}^{!}\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\curly P_{X}}\in K_{0}(\curly P_{Y},\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2}]),

where
\imath_{*}\widehat{\imath}^{!}(-)=(-)\cdot\widehat{\Lambda}^{\bullet}(\curly L)^{\vee}
by
[
Par
, Lem. 4.11, Lem. B.6]
.
∎

The following cohomological conditions guarantee that A1), A2) are satisfied.

Proposition 4.3
.

Let
X
be a projective Calabi-Yau 4-fold and
\iota:Y\hookrightarrow X
a smooth connected effective divisor. Let
q\in\{-1,0,1\}
and
v\in H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})
. Let
\curly P_{Y}:=\curly P_{v}^{(q)}(Y)
,
\curly P_{X}:=\curly P_{v}^{(q)}(X)
, and
L:=\mathcal{O}_{X}(Y)
. Assume the following:

\mathrm{(1)}

H^{>0}(X,F\otimes L)=0
for all closed points
(F,s)\in\curly P_{X}
.

\mathrm{(2)}

H^{2}(Y,F)=0
for all closed points
(F,s)\in\curly P_{Y}
.

Then A1) and A2) of Theorem
4.1
are satisfied.

Proof.

A1) is satisfied by cohomology and base change. Denote by
\mathbb{I}_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}=[\mathcal{O}\to\mathbb{F}_{Y}]
the universal complex on
Y\times\curly P_{Y}
and consider the complexes

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{Y}

\displaystyle:=(R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi_{\curly P_{Y}}}(\mathbb{I}_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\mathbb{I}_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}[1])^{\vee},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{Y}

\displaystyle:=(R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi_{\curly P_{Y}}}(\mathbb{I}_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\mathbb{F}_{Y}))^{\vee}.

There is a natural exact triangle

(15)

R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi_{\curly P_{Y}}}(\mathbb{I}_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\mathbb{F}_{Y})\to R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi_{\curly P_{Y}}}(\mathbb{I}_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},\mathbb{I}_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}[1]\to R\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}_{\pi_{\curly P_{Y}}}(\mathbb{F}_{Y},\mathcal{O})[2]

which induces a map
\mathbb{E}_{Y}\to\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{Y}
. This map fits in the commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{E}_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\mathbb{E}^{\prime}_{Y}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{L}_{\curly P_{Y}},}

induced by the canonical map from the derived moduli of pairs on
Y
to the derived moduli of perfect complexes on
Y
, see e.g.
[
BKP
, Rem. 3.9]
.
In particular,
\psi^{\prime}
is an obstruction theory. Since
Y\times\curly P_{Y}
is projective (Proposition
2.8
), the complex
\mathbb{I}_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
is perfect. Therefore, the reasoning in the proof of
[
PT1
, Lem. 2.10]
shows that
\mathbb{E}_{Y}
is a perfect complex. Since
K_{Y}=L|_{Y}
, condition (1) implies
R^{>0}\pi_{\curly P_{Y}*}(\mathbb{F}_{Y}\otimes K_{Y})=0
. The dual of (
15
) and the commutative diagram imply that
\psi
is an obstruction theory.

Next, we have to show that
\mathbb{E}_{Y}
is 2-term, for which it suffices to prove that
\operatorname{Ext}^{i}_{Y}(I_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}=0
for
i\neq 1,2
for any
I_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}=[\mathcal{O}_{Y}\to F]\in\curly P_{Y}
. Consider the absolute version of (
15
), i.e.

\displaystyle R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(I_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},F)\to R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(I_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}[1]\to R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(F,\mathcal{O}_{Y})[2].

Using the fact that
I_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
is a
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pair, it is easy to show that
\operatorname{Ext}^{<0}_{Y}(I_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},F)=0
(see
[
BKP
, Lem. 3.20]
and its proof). Moreover,
\operatorname{Ext}_{Y}^{<2}(F,\mathcal{O}_{Y})\cong H^{>1}(Y,F\otimes K_{Y})^{*}\cong H^{>1}(X,F\otimes L)^{*}\cong 0
, thus
\operatorname{Ext}^{<1}_{Y}(I_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}=0
. Using the same exact triangle twisted by
K_{Y}
, and condition (2), we find
\operatorname{Ext}^{>2}_{Y}(I_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}=0
.
∎

We now relate the
surface
counting on the smooth projective 3-fold
Y
to
curve
counting on
Y
.
First, we recall curve counting on
Y
. For
q\in\{-1,0\}
,
v=(0,0,\beta,n-\beta\cdot\mathrm{td}_{1}(Y))\in H^{*}(Y,\mathbb{Q})
, we denote by
\curly P_{v}^{(q)}(Y)

the moduli space parametrizing pairs
(F,s)
such that
F
is a coherent sheaf on
Y
satisfying
\operatorname{ch}(F)=v
, and
s:\mathcal{O}_{Y}\to F
is a section satisfying the following
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
stability condition

F\in\mathrm{Coh}_{\geq q+1}(Y)Q:=\operatorname{coker}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}\xrightarrow{s}F)\in\mathrm{Coh}_{\leq q}(Y).

It is well-known that
\curly P:=\curly P_{v}^{(q)}(Y)
is a fine projective moduli space. For
q=-1
, these are the Hilbert schemes of curves on
Y

[
Gro
]
, and for
q=0
, they are the moduli spaces of Pandharipande-Thomas pairs
[
Pot1
,
Pot2
,
PT1
]
. Moreover,
\curly P
has a perfect obstruction theory with accompanying virtual cycle and twisted virtual structure sheaf
[
BF
,
Tho1
,
PT1
]

\displaystyle[\curly P]^{\mathrm{vir}}\in A_{\mathrm{vd}}(\curly P),\quad\mathrm{vd}=\beta\cdot c_{1}(Y)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\curly P}:=\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\curly P}\otimes(\det(T_{\curly P}^{\mathrm{vir}}))^{-\frac{1}{2}}\in K_{0}(\curly P,\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2}]),

where
(\det(T_{\curly P}^{\mathrm{vir}}))^{-\frac{1}{2}}
is the Nekrasov-Okounkov twist
[
NO
]
.
8
8

8

Existence of square roots of
\det(T_{\curly P}^{\mathrm{vir}})
, as a line bundle, is discussed in
[
NO
, Sect. 6]
, but does not concern us here in this section.
The
K
-theoretic
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
invariants are defined by

\langle\!\langle 1\rangle\!\rangle_{Y,v}^{\mathrm{PT}_{q}}:=\chi\big(\curly P,\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\curly P}\big)\in\mathbb{Q}.

We also denote these invariants by
\langle\!\langle 1\rangle\!\rangle_{Y,\beta,n}^{\mathrm{PT}_{q}}
and we sometimes write
\mathrm{DT}:=\mathrm{PT}_{-1}
and
\mathrm{PT}:=\mathrm{PT}_{0}
. For background, we refer to
[
MNOP
,
PT2
,
Oko
,
NO
]
. Then the
K
-theoretic
\mathrm{DT}
–
\mathrm{PT}
correspondence states
[
NO
]

(16)

\frac{\sum_{n}\langle\!\langle 1\rangle\!\rangle_{Y,\beta,n}^{\mathrm{DT}}q^{n}}{\sum_{n}\langle\!\langle 1\rangle\!\rangle_{Y,0,n}^{\mathrm{DT}}q^{n}}=\sum_{n}\langle\!\langle 1\rangle\!\rangle_{Y,\beta,n}^{\mathrm{PT}}q^{n}.

For
Y
a Calabi-Yau 3-fold, the virtual dimension of
\curly P
is zero, and we have
\langle\!\langle 1\rangle\!\rangle_{Y,\beta,n}^{\mathrm{PT}_{q}}=\int_{[\curly P]^{\mathrm{vir}}}1
. Then (
16
) was proved by Bridgeland
[
Bri
]
, and Toda
[
Tod
]
in the Euler characteristic case. For
Y
a toric 3-fold, the virtual dimension of
\curly P
may be positive, and the correspondence was recently proved by Kuhn-Liu-Thimm
[
KLT
]
.

Remark 4.4
.

It is a useful convention to declare
[\mathcal{O}_{Y}\to 0]
to be the unique
\mathrm{PT}
pair on
Y
with
\beta=n=0
. We then set
\curly P_{v}^{(0)}(Y):=\operatorname{Spec}\,\mathbb{C}
and
\langle\!\langle 1\rangle\!\rangle_{Y,0,0}^{\mathrm{PT}}:=1=1/\widehat{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(0)
.
This convention makes sense in view of (
16
).

Corollary 4.5
.

Let
S\subset Y\subset X
, where
X
is a projective Calabi-Yau 4-fold,
Y
is a smooth connected 3-fold, and
S
is a smooth connected surface. Suppose
h^{0}(N_{S/Y})=h^{1}(N_{S/Y})=h^{2}(\mathcal{O}_{S})=0
, and
S
is the only pure 2-dimensional subscheme in class
[S]\in H^{2}(Y,\mathbb{Q})
. Suppose furthermore that the inclusion map
\iota:Y\hookrightarrow X
induces an injection
H_{*}(Y,\mathbb{Q})\hookrightarrow H_{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})
. Let
v^{\prime}\in H^{*}(Y,\mathbb{Q})
and
v=(0,0,\gamma,\beta,n-\mathrm{td}_{2}(X)\cdot\gamma)=\iota_{*}v^{\prime}\cdot\mathrm{td}(L)^{-1}
where
L:=\mathcal{O}_{X}(Y)
. Let
v^{\prime\prime}:=1-(1-v^{\prime})e^{[S]}\in H^{*}(Y,\mathbb{Q})
, and set
\beta^{\prime\prime}:=v_{2}^{\prime\prime}\in H^{4}(Y,\mathbb{Q})
. Suppose for any 2-dimensional closed subscheme
Z\subset X
satisfying
\operatorname{ch}_{2}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})=\gamma
and
\operatorname{ch}_{3}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})=\beta
, we have

H^{1}(X,(T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})/T_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{Z}))\otimes L)=0,

where
T_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\subset T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\subset\mathcal{O}_{Z}
is the torsion filtration of
\mathcal{O}_{Z}
. Finally suppose that, for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, the inclusion maps
\curly P^{(q)}_{v^{\prime}}(Y)\hookrightarrow\curly P^{(q)}_{v}(X)
for
q\in\{-1,0\}
induce injective maps on the sets of connected components.

Then, for
y=1
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\langle\!\langle L\rangle\!\rangle_{X,\gamma,\beta,n}^{\mathrm{DT}}q^{n}

\displaystyle=q^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\sum_{n}\langle\!\langle 1\rangle\!\rangle_{Y,\beta^{\prime\prime},n}^{\mathrm{DT}}q^{n},

\displaystyle\sum_{n}\langle\!\langle L\rangle\!\rangle_{X,\gamma,\beta,n}^{\mathrm{PT}_{0}}q^{n}

\displaystyle=q^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\sum_{n}\langle\!\langle 1\rangle\!\rangle_{Y,\beta^{\prime\prime},n}^{\mathrm{PT}}q^{n},

where
q^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
is and overall factor with power depending only on
S,Y,X,\beta^{\prime\prime}
. Moreover, the
\mathrm{DT}
–
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
correspondence for
X
,
L
,
\gamma
,
\beta
,
y=1
is equivalent to the
\mathrm{DT}
–
\mathrm{PT}
correspondence for
Y
,
\beta^{\prime\prime}
. Thus it holds in the following three cases:

•

when
\beta^{\prime\prime}=0
(cf. Remark
4.4
);

•

when
Y
is a Calabi-Yau 3-fold by
[
Bri
,
Tod
]
;

•

when
Y
is a toric 3-fold by
[
KLT
]
.

Proof.

Let
q\in\{-1,0\}
. For any
(F,s)\in{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
with cokernel
Q
and scheme theoretic support
Z
, we have short exact sequences

\displaystyle 0\to\mathcal{O}_{Z}\to F\to Q\to 0

\displaystyle 0\to T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\to\mathcal{O}_{Z}\to\mathcal{O}_{S}\to 0,

from which (1) and (2) of Proposition
4.3
are easily deduced. For the chosen cohomology classes, we have morphisms

\curly P_{v^{\prime\prime}}^{(q)}(Y)\to\curly P_{v^{\prime}}^{(q)}(Y),\quad J^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\mapsto J^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(-S)=I_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}.

Since
S
is the only pure 2-dimensional subscheme in
\gamma
, these are isomorphisms by Proposition
2.5
. Moreover, the universal sheaves are related by
\mathbb{J}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(-S)\cong\mathbb{I}_{Y}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}
. Hence the isomorphisms preserve the virtual cycles, from which the two equalities in the proposition follow.

The assumption on connected components implies that we can choose orientations so that all signs
(-1)^{\sigma(i)}
in (
14
) are
+1
. Thus the statement on the
\mathrm{DT}
–
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
correspondence follows from the fact that for
y=1
, we have

\sum_{n}\langle\!\langle L\rangle\!\rangle_{X,0,0,n}^{\mathrm{DT}}q^{n}=\sum_{n}\langle\!\langle 1\rangle\!\rangle_{Y,0,n}^{\mathrm{DT}}q^{n},

which was proved by third-named author
[
Par
]
.
∎

In the following two examples, we use the notation of Corollary
4.5
and we assume that the condition on connected components in the corollary is satisfied.

Example 4.6
.

Let
Y\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a general Weierstraß fibration with unique section
\mathbb{P}^{2}\subset Y
. Let
X=Y\times E
, where
E
is a smooth elliptic curve. Let
\gamma=[\mathbb{P}^{2}]
and
\beta^{\prime\prime}=\epsilon[\mathbb{P}^{1}]
, where
\mathbb{P}^{1}\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
\epsilon=0,1
. Then the assumptions of Corollary
4.5
are satisfied.

Example 4.7
.

Let
Y
be the blow-up of
\mathbb{P}^{3}
in a point and let
\mathbb{P}^{2}\subset Y
be the class of the exceptional divisor. Let
X\to Y
be a general Weierstraß fibration with section, so we have
\mathbb{P}^{2}\subset Y\subset X
. Let
\gamma=[\mathbb{P}^{2}]
and
\beta^{\prime\prime}=\epsilon[\mathbb{P}^{1}]
, where
\mathbb{P}^{1}\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
\epsilon=0,1
. Then the assumptions of Corollary
4.5
are satisfied.

4.2.
Via virtual localization

Let
Y
be a smooth quasi-projective toric 3-fold. Denote the dense open torus of
Y
by
T_{Y}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{3}
. For
q\in\{-1,0\}
,
v=(0,0,\beta,n-\beta\cdot\mathrm{td}_{1}(Y))\in H^{*}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})
, let
\curly P:=\curly P_{v}^{(q)}(Y)
. Similar to the previous section,
\curly P
has a perfect obstruction theory with accompanying
equivariant
virtual class and twisted virtual structure sheaf

\displaystyle[\curly P]^{\mathrm{vir}}\in A_{\mathrm{vd}}^{T_{Y}}(\curly P),\quad\mathrm{vd}=\beta\cdot c_{1}(Y)

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\curly P}:=\mathcal{O}^{\mathrm{vir}}_{\curly P}\otimes(\det(T_{\curly P}^{\mathrm{vir}}))^{-\frac{1}{2}}\in K_{0}^{T_{Y}}(\curly P,\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2}]).

The action of
T_{Y}
on
Y
lifts to
\curly P
. Then the fixed locus
\curly P^{T_{Y}}
is proper.
9
9

9

For
q=-1
the fixed locus is 0-dimensional reduced
[
MNOP
]
, and for
q=0
it is a disjoint union of products of
\mathbb{P}^{1}

[
PT2
]
.
The
K
-theoretic
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
invariants are defined by

\langle\!\langle 1\rangle\!\rangle_{Y,v}^{\mathrm{PT}_{q}}:=\chi\big(\curly P,\widehat{\mathcal{O}}^{\mathrm{vir}}\big)\in\mathbb{Q}(t^{\frac{1}{2}}_{1},t^{\frac{1}{2}}_{2},t^{\frac{1}{2}}_{3}),

where
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}
are the equivariant parameters of
T_{Y}
. We also denote these invariants by
\langle\!\langle 1\rangle\!\rangle_{Y,\beta,n}^{\mathrm{PT}_{q}}
. Abstractly, these invariants are defined by Thomason localization as in Section
2.3
, and concretely they are given by the Graber-Pandharipande localization formula
[
GP
]
(or its
K
-theoretic version due to Qu
[
Qu
]
). Then the equivariant
K
-theoretic
\mathrm{DT}
–
\mathrm{PT}
correspondence is given by the same formula (
16
) and was recently established in
[
KLT
]
.

We now consider the toric Calabi-Yau 4-fold

p:X=\mathrm{Tot}(K_{Y})\to Y,

i.e., the total space of the canonical line bundle on
Y
. Let
T\leq T_{X}:=T_{Y}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
be the algebraic torus preserving the Calabi-Yau volume form, then
T\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{3}\cong T_{Y}
. Let
v=(0,0,\gamma,\beta,n-\gamma\cdot\mathrm{td}_{2}(X))\in H_{c}^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})
and consider
\curly P:=\curly P_{v}^{(q)}(X)
. We suppose the following:

Assumption.

\curly P^{T}
is 0-dimensional and reduced.

As we will see in Section
6
, there are numerous examples where this assumption is satisfied, e.g., it is always satisfied for
q=-1
. We consider the
T
-equivariant line bundle
L:=\mathcal{O}_{X}(Y)
. In Definition
3.13
, we defined the
T
-equivariant
K
-theoretic invariants

\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(Y)\rangle\!\rangle_{X,v}^{\mathrm{PT}_{q}},\quad q\in\{-1,0,1\}.

Let
\mathcal{E}
be a
T
-equivariant coherent sheaf with proper support on
X
and define
E:=p_{*}\mathcal{E}
. Then there exists a distinguished triangle
[
TT
, Prop. 2.14]

R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})\to R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(E,E)\to R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(E,E\otimes K_{Y}).

Therefore, in
K
-theory, we have a splitting

(17)

R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{E})=R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(E,E)+R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(E,E)^{\vee}\in K_{0}^{T}(\mathrm{pt}).

Serre duality in this setting is justified by Section
2.2
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}=[\mathcal{O}_{X}\to F]\in\curly P^{T}
. Then

(\sqrt{\mathfrak{e}}(T_{\curly P}^{\mathrm{vir}}|_{I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}}))^{2}=(\widehat{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(R\Gamma(Y,p_{*}F)-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(p_{*}F,p_{*}F))^{\vee})^{2}.

Proof.

Applying (
17
) to
\mathcal{E}=F
, we obtain the following identity in
K_{0}^{T}(\mathrm{pt})

(18)

\displaystyle\begin{split}T_{\curly P}^{\mathrm{vir}}|_{I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}}&=-R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})_{0}\\
&=R\Gamma(X,F)+R\Gamma(X,F)^{\vee}-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(p_{*}F,p_{*}F)-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(p_{*}F,p_{*}F)^{\vee}.\end{split}

The result follows by combining with Proposition
2.13
and using the fact that for any complex we have
\widehat{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}V=(-1)^{\operatorname{rk}V}\widehat{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}V^{\vee}
.
∎

We therefore have

(19)

\langle\!\langle L\rangle\!\rangle_{X,v}^{\mathrm{PT}_{q}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}=[\mathcal{O}_{X}\to F]\in\curly P^{T}\\
\mathrm{vd}(\{I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\})=0\end{subarray}}(-1)^{\sigma_{I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}}}\frac{\widehat{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(R\Gamma(X,F(Y))\otimes y^{-1})}{\widehat{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(R\Gamma(Y,p_{*}F)-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(p_{*}F,p_{*}F))^{\vee}},

which depends on a choice of sign attached to each
T
-fixed point. Henceforth, we make any choice satisfying the following:

Choice.
For any
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\in\curly P^{T}
corresponding to a
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pair which is scheme theoretically supported on the zero section
Y\subset X
, we take
(-1)^{\sigma_{I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}}}=1
.

Remark 4.9
.

We expect that for any choice of (global) orientation on
\curly P
, we have
(-1)^{\sigma_{I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}}}=(-1)^{\sigma_{J^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}}}
for any two
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},J^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\in\curly P^{T}
corresponding to
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pairs supported on the zero section
Y\subset X
. This can be seen as a toric analog of the expectation discussed below (
14
). In other words, we expect that for signs coming from (global) orientations on
\curly P
, the choice made above is automatic (up to overall sign).

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
Y
be a smooth quasi-projective toric 3-fold and consider
p:X=\mathrm{Tot}(K_{Y})\to Y
. Denote by
\iota:Y\hookrightarrow X
the inclusion of the zero section and by
T\leq T_{X}=T_{Y}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
the subtorus preserving the Calabi-Yau volume form. Fix
v\in H^{*}_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q})
, and let
v^{\prime}\in H^{*}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})
be the unique class determined by the equation
\iota_{*}v^{\prime}=v\cdot\mathrm{td}(\mathcal{O}_{X}(Y))
. Let
q\in\{-1,0,1\}
and assume the following conditions hold:

\mathrm{B1)}

\curly P_{v}^{(q)}(X)^{T}
is 0-dimensional and reduced.

\mathrm{B2)}

For any
(F,s)\in\curly P_{v}^{(q)}(X)^{T}

not
scheme-theoretically supported on
Y\subset X
, the class

R\Gamma(X,F(Y))\in K^{T}_{0}(\mathrm{pt})
has a
positive

T
-fixed term.

\mathrm{B3)}

There exists an effective
T_{Y}
-invariant divisor
10
10

10

Recall that any effective divisor
S=\sum_{i}n_{i}D_{i}
on
Y
, where

D_{i}
are prime divisors, gives rise to a scheme
S\subset Y
defined by the ideal
\prod_{i}I_{D_{i}/Y}^{n_{i}}
.

\jmath:S\hookrightarrow Y
with proper support, such that for all
(F,s)\in\curly P_{v^{\prime}}^{(q)}(Y)^{T_{Y}}
we have
S=\mathrm{Supp}(F)^{\mathrm{pure}}
.

Then, for
q\in\{-1,0\}
,
\lim_{y\to 1}\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(Y)\rangle\!\rangle_{X,v}^{\mathrm{PT}_{q}}
is well-defined and

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to 1}\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(Y)\rangle\!\rangle_{X,v}^{\mathrm{DT}}

\displaystyle=\langle\!\langle 1\rangle\!\rangle_{Y,v^{\prime\prime}}^{\mathrm{DT}},

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to 1}\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(Y)\rangle\!\rangle_{X,v}^{\mathrm{PT}_{0}}

\displaystyle=\langle\!\langle 1\rangle\!\rangle_{Y,v^{\prime\prime}}^{\mathrm{PT}},

where
v^{\prime\prime}=1-(1-v^{\prime})\cdot\exp(v^{\prime}_{1})
and
v^{\prime}_{1}
denotes the component of
v^{\prime}
in
H^{2}_{c}(Y,\mathbb{Q})
.

For
q=1
,
\lim_{y\to 1}\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(Y)\rangle\!\rangle_{X,v}^{\mathrm{PT}_{1}}
is well-defined and for
S
sufficiently ample
11
11

11

The required “sufficient ampleness” is defined in the proof.
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{y\to 1}\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(Y)\rangle\!\rangle_{X,v}^{\mathrm{PT}_{1}}=\langle\!\langle 1\rangle\!\rangle_{Y,v^{\prime\prime\prime}}^{\mathrm{PT}},

where
v^{\prime\prime\prime}=(v^{\prime})^{\vee}\cdot(\operatorname{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}(S))/\mathrm{td}(\mathcal{O}_{Y}(S)))^{-1}+\jmath_{*}\operatorname{ch}(\mathcal{O}_{S})
.

Proof.

We are concerned with the calculation of (
19
). Note that we may drop the requirement
\mathrm{vd}(\{I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\})=0
. Indeed, if
\mathrm{vd}(\{I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\})<0
, the complexes
T_{\curly P}^{\mathrm{vir}}|_{I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}}
and

R\Gamma(Y,p_{*}F)-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(p_{*}F,p_{*}F)

have a negative
T
-fixed term (by (
18
)), thus contributing zero to (
19
).

By assumption B2), we can also restrict attention to
T
-fixed pairs which are scheme theoretically supported on
Y
, i.e., they are in the image of

\curly P_{v^{\prime}}^{(q)}(Y)^{T_{Y}}\hookrightarrow\curly P_{v}^{(q)}(X)^{T}.

Such a pair is of the form
[\mathcal{O}_{X}\to\iota_{*}F]
for some
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pair
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}=[\mathcal{O}_{Y}\to F]
, where the map
\mathcal{O}_{X}\to\iota_{*}F
is the composition
\mathcal{O}_{X}\to\iota_{*}\mathcal{O}_{Y}\to\iota_{*}F
. Since
p_{*}((\iota_{*}F)(Y))\cong F\otimes K_{Y}
, we are interested in

R\Gamma(Y,F)-R\Gamma(Y,F\otimes K_{Y})^{\vee}y-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(F,F),

which, for
y=1
, equals
-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})_{0}
by Serre duality on
Y
. In each of the cases
q\in\{-1,0,1\}
, we show below that
-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})_{0}
has no positive
T_{Y}
fixed part. Then the limit in the theorem is well-defined and, by Choice, we have

(20)

\lim_{y\to 1}\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(Y)\rangle\!\rangle_{X,v}^{\mathrm{PT}_{q}}=\sum_{I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\in\curly P_{v^{\prime}}(Y)^{T_{Y}}}\frac{1}{\widehat{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}[1])^{\vee}}.

Our remaining task is to rewrite the complex
R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}[1]
in each of the cases
q\in\{-1,0,1\}
and show in the process that it has no positive
T_{Y}
-fixed part. Take
I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\in\curly P_{v^{\prime}}(Y)^{T_{Y}}
and denote the scheme-theoretic support of
I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}
by
Z\subset Y
and let
Z^{\mathrm{pure}}
be the underlying effective divisor. By B3), we have
Z^{\mathrm{pure}}=S
.

\mathrm{DT}
case.
For
q=-1
, we have

I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}\cong I_{Z/Y}\cong I_{C/Y}(-S),

where
C\subset Y
has dimension
\leq 1
. We deduce the following equality in
K_{0}^{T}(\mathrm{pt})\cong K_{0}^{T_{Y}}(\mathrm{pt})

(21)

-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})_{0}=-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(I_{C/Y},I_{C/Y})_{0}.

For
v^{\prime\prime}
as defined in the theorem, consider the following bijection (at the level of
\mathbb{C}
-valued points)

\curly P_{v^{\prime}}^{(-1)}(Y)^{T_{Y}}\to\curly P_{v^{\prime\prime}}^{(-1)}(Y)^{T_{Y}},\quad I_{Z/Y}\mapsto I_{Z/Y}(S).

Moreover, by
[
MNOP
, Lem. 6 & 8]
,
\curly P_{v^{\prime\prime}}^{(-1)}(Y)^{T_{Y}}
is 0-dimensional and reduced, and the complex

has no
T_{Y}
-fixed part. This establishes the
q=-1
case by (
20
) and (
21
).

\mathrm{PT}_{0}
case.
For
q=0
, by Proposition
2.5
, we have

I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}\cong J^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(-S),\quad J^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}:=[\mathcal{O}_{Y}\to T_{1}(F)(S)],

where
J^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}
is a
\mathrm{PT}
pair on
Y
.
We obtain the following equality in
K_{0}^{T_{Y}}(\mathrm{pt})

(22)

-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})_{0}=-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(J^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},J^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})_{0}.

For
v^{\prime\prime}
as defined in the theorem, consider the map (at the level of
\mathbb{C}
-valued points)

\curly P_{v^{\prime}}^{(0)}(Y)^{T_{Y}}\to\curly P_{v^{\prime\prime}}^{(0)}(Y)^{T_{Y}},\quad I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}\mapsto I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}(S).

This map is a bijection by Proposition
2.5
and B3).
12
12

12

Note that, by Remark
4.4
, we allow the case
T_{1}(F)=0
.

By B1),
\curly P_{v^{\prime\prime}}^{(0)}(Y)^{T_{Y}}
is 0-dimensional. Moreover, by
[
PT2
, Thm. 1]
it is reduced and therefore
R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(J^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},J^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})_{0}[1]
has no positive
T_{Y}
-fixed part. The
q=0
case then follows by (
20
) and (
22
).

\mathrm{PT}_{1}
case.
For
q=1
, by Proposition
2.6
, we have

F^{\vee}\otimes K_{Y}[1]\cong\jmath_{*}(J^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}\otimes K_{S}),

where
J^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}=[\mathcal{O}_{S}\to G]
is a
\mathrm{PT}
pair on
S
and
\jmath_{*}(G\otimes K_{S})=Q^{D}
. We first write

-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})_{0}=R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(F^{\vee},\mathcal{O}_{Y})+R\Gamma(Y,F^{\vee})-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(F^{\vee},F^{\vee}).

Using the substitutions

\displaystyle F^{\vee}

\displaystyle=-\jmath_{*}(K_{S})\otimes K_{Y}^{-1}+\jmath_{*}(G\otimes K_{S})\otimes K_{Y}^{-1},

\displaystyle K_{S}

\displaystyle=K_{Y}(S)|_{S},\quad\mathcal{O}_{S}=\mathcal{O}_{Y}-\mathcal{O}_{Y}(-S),

we find

(23)

\displaystyle\begin{split}-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})_{0}&=-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(J^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},J^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})_{0}-R\Gamma(Y,\mathcal{O}_{S}(S))-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(\mathcal{O}_{S},\mathcal{O}_{Y})\\
&=-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(J^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},J^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})_{0}.\end{split}

For the second equality, we used Serre duality on
Y
and
S
(
S
is Gorenstein with proper support):

-R\operatorname{Hom}_{Y}(\mathcal{O}_{S},\mathcal{O}_{Y})=R\Gamma(S,K_{S}(-S))^{\vee}=R\Gamma(S,\mathcal{O}_{S}(S)).

For
v^{\prime\prime\prime}
as defined in the theorem, consider the map (at the level of
\mathbb{C}
-valued points)

\curly P_{v^{\prime}}^{(1)}(Y)^{T_{Y}}\to\curly P_{v^{\prime\prime\prime}}^{(0)}(Y)^{T_{Y}},\quad I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}\mapsto[\mathcal{O}_{Y}\to\jmath_{*}G],

where
G
was defined above.
13
13

13

Note that, by Remark
4.4
, we allow the case
Q=0
, i.e.,
G=0
.
By Proposition
2.6
, this is a bijection onto the locus of
\mathrm{PT}
pairs scheme theoretically supported on
S
. By definition, we say that
S
is sufficiently ample when this map is surjective. Then the proof follows from (

) and (
23
)
∎

Let
L_{1},L_{2}
be line bundles on a smooth projective toric surface
S
, such that
L_{1}\otimes L_{2}\cong K_{S}
, and
Y=\mathrm{Tot}(L_{1})
. Then
X=\mathrm{Tot}(L_{1}\oplus L_{2})
and, taking
\gamma=d[S]
for any
d>0
, condition B3) is automatic. We establish in Theorem
5.29
that condition B1) is satisfied for
q\in\{-1,0\}
and it conjecturally holds for
q=1
too (Conjecture
5.30
). Hence, for local surfaces, the main condition is B2).

Suppose
S
is del Pezzo and
Z\subset X
is a
T
-fixed 2-dimensional proper closed subscheme with class
\gamma
and
no embedded curves
, i.e., its torsion filtration
T_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\subset T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\subset\mathcal{O}_{Z}
satisfies
T_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})=T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})
. Using push-forward along the projection
p:X\to S
, it is not hard to show that if
Z
is not scheme-theoretically supported on the zero section
Y\subset X
, then
R\Gamma(X,L_{2}|_{Z})
has positive
T
-fixed part so B2) is satisfied.

Suppose
S=\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
L_{1}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-a)
,
L_{2}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-3+a)
for any
a\geq 1
. We claim that for
q=1
, condition B2) is always satisfied. Indeed, for a
T
-fixed
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
pair on
X
with proper support and class
\gamma
, we have a short exact sequence
0\to\mathcal{O}_{Z}\to F\to Q\to 0
, where
Z\subset X
is pure 2-dimensional. Thus, by the previous paragraph, it suffices to show that
R\Gamma(X,Q\otimes L_{2})
does not have negative
T
-fixed part. This follows from the toric description of
T
-fixed points in Section
5.2
. Taking
a=3
and
d\gg 0
, Theorem
4.10
implies that the
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
invariants recover the refined
\mathrm{PT}
invariants of
Y=\mathrm{Tot}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-3))
studied by Choi-Katz-Klemm
[
CKK
]
.

Condition B2) is not always satisfied. Continuing with
S=\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
X=\mathrm{Tot}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-a)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-3+a))
, let
C:=Z(x_{0}x_{1}x_{2})\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}
and consider the
T
-fixed 2-dimensional subscheme
Z\subset X
with reduced support
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and determined by the short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{C}\otimes L_{2}^{-1}\to\mathcal{O}_{Z}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\to 0,

where
\mathcal{O}_{Z}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
denotes restriction to the reduced support. Since
R\Gamma(\mathbb{P}^{2},\mathcal{O}_{C})=0
, the
K
-theory class
R\Gamma(X,L_{2}|_{Z})
does not have positive
T
-fixed part. However, for a
T
-fixed
\mathrm{DT}
or
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
pair
(F,s)
on
X
with proper support and class
\gamma=d[S]
(for any
d>0
) and scheme theoretic support
Z
satisfying
\operatorname{ch}_{3}(T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z}))=[\mathbb{P}^{1}]
or
2[\mathbb{P}^{1}]
, the toric description of
T
-fixed points in Section
5.2
can be used to show that
R\Gamma(X,F\otimes L_{2})
has a positive
T
-fixed part.

Example 4.11
.

Let
X=\mathrm{Tot}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-a)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-3+a))
and
Y=\mathrm{Tot}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-a))
for any
a\leq 1
. Fix
\gamma=d[\mathbb{P}^{2}]
for any
d>0
and
\beta=(\epsilon+\tfrac{3}{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}d(d-1)a)[\mathbb{P}^{1}]
with
\epsilon\in\{0,1,2\}
. Then for any
v=(0,0,\gamma,\beta,*)\in H^{*}_{c}(X,\mathbb{Q})
, we are in the setting of the previous paragraph and the conditions of Theorem
4.10
are satisfied for
q\in\{-1,0\}
. Hence, for
L=\mathcal{O}_{X}(Y)
and
y=1
, the
\mathrm{DT}
–
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
correspondence reduces to the
K
-theoretic
\mathrm{DT}
–
\mathrm{PT}
correspondence for
Y
which was proved in
[
KLT
]
.
14
14

14

In this example, we use that for
y=1
,
\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}(Y)\rangle\!\rangle_{X,0,0,n}^{\mathrm{DT}}=\langle\!\langle 1\rangle\!\rangle_{Y,0,n}^{\mathrm{DT}}
which is known to hold by the 0-dimensional analog of Proposition
6.33
(cf.
[
KR
]
). See also Remark
6.11
.
In particular, Conjecture
3.14
holds in this setting.

5.
Fixed loci

In this section, for
X
a toric Calabi-Yau 4-fold, we discuss the fixed loci of the moduli spaces
{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
and the representations of tangent spaces at the fixed points.

5.1.
Toric geometry

For any smooth quasi-projective toric 4-fold
X
, we denote its dense algebraic torus by
T_{X}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
.
We denote by
V(X)
its collection of
T_{X}
-fixed points, by
E(X)
its collection of
proper

T_{X}
-fixed irreducible 1-dimensional subvarieties (which are “
\mathbb{P}^{1}
’s”), and by
F(X)
its collection of
proper

T_{X}
-fixed irreducible 2-dimensional subvarieties (which are irreducible smooth projective toric surfaces). We refer to elements of
V(X)
,
E(X)
,
F(X)
as vertices, edges, and faces respectively.

The elements
\alpha\in V(X)
correspond to
T_{X}
-fixed points
p_{\alpha}\in X
. Since these fixed points correspond to 4-dimensional cones in the fan, they are also in bijection with
T_{X}
-invariant affine open subsets
U_{\alpha}\subset X
. For each
\alpha\in V(X)
, there exist coordinate functions
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}
on
U_{\alpha}
such that the action of
T_{X}
is given by

(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4})\cdot(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})=(t_{1}x_{1},t_{2}x_{2},t_{3}x_{3},t_{4}x_{4}).

If
X
is Calabi-Yau, then the subtorus

(24)

T=Z(t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}t_{4}-1)\leq T_{X}

preserves the Calabi-Yau volume form, and we refer to
T
as the
Calabi-Yau torus
.
Using labels
\alpha
for the vertices, we can label edges by
\alpha\beta
according to the vertices which they connect. Any edge
\alpha\beta\in E(X)
corresponds to a
T_{X}
-fixed line
\mathbb{P}^{1}\cong L_{\alpha\beta}\subset X
with normal bundle

(25)

\displaystyle N_{L_{\alpha\beta}/X}\cong\mathcal{O}(m_{\alpha\beta})\oplus\mathcal{O}(m_{\alpha\beta}^{\prime})\oplus\mathcal{O}(m_{\alpha\beta}^{\prime\prime}).

When
X
is Calabi-Yau, we have

m_{\alpha\beta}+m_{\alpha\beta}^{\prime}+m_{\alpha\beta}^{\prime\prime}=-2.

For coordinates
x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}
on
U_{\alpha}
and
x_{1}^{\prime},x_{2}^{\prime},x_{3}^{\prime},x_{4}^{\prime}
on
U_{\beta}
such that
L_{\alpha\beta}\cap U_{\alpha}=Z(x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
and
L_{\alpha\beta}\cap U_{\beta}=Z(x_{2}^{\prime},x_{3}^{\prime},x_{4}^{\prime})
, the coordinate transformation from
U_{\alpha}
to
U_{\beta}
is given by

(26)

(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})\mapsto(x_{1}^{-1},x_{1}^{-m_{\alpha\beta}}x_{2},x_{1}^{-m_{\alpha\beta}^{\prime}}x_{3},x_{1}^{-m_{\alpha\beta}^{\prime\prime}}x_{4}).

Faces
f\in F(X)
correspond to irreducible smooth projective toric surfaces
S_{f}
. The normal bundle
N_{S_{f}/X}
is a (split!) rank 2 equivariant bundle on
S_{f}
.
Note that any smooth projective toric surface can arise in this way.

In this section, we use partitions in various dimensions.

Definition 5.1
.

Let
\mu\subset\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{d}
. We say that
\mu
is a
d
-dimensional partition when

(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})\in\mu\Rightarrow(j_{1},\ldots,j_{d})\in\mu\quad\forall(j_{1},\ldots,j_{d})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{d}\,\textrm{with}\,j_{k}\leq i_{k}\,\textrm{for all}\,k.

For
d=2,3,4
, we refer to
\mu
as a
partition
,
plane partition
,
solid partition
respectively. A partition is called finite when
\mu
is a finite set, in which case its
size

|\mu|
is the cardinality of this set.

A partition
\mu
can also be written as a sequence

\mu=\{\mu(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d-1})\}_{i_{1},\ldots,i_{d-1}\geq 0}

determined by

(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d})\in\mu\Leftrightarrow i_{d}\leq\mu(i_{1},\ldots,i_{d-1}).

In this way, solid partitions can be visualized as plane partitions in
\mathbb{R}^{3}
, where each box located at
(i,j,k)\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{3}
carries a number
\mu(i,j,k)\geq 0
indicating how many boxes are stacked “on top of it” in the positive
x_{4}
-direction. Note that the number
\mu(i,j,k)
can be infinite.

Example 5.2
.

In Figure
2
, we give a graphical representation of the solid partition corresponding to the scheme theoretic union of the schemes cut out by
(x_{3}^{2},x_{4})
and
(x_{1}^{2},x_{2}^{2})
.
The blue boxes extend infinitely far into the first quadrant of the
x_{1}x_{2}
-plane. The blue boxes have height
0
in the positive
x_{4}
-direction. The purple boxes extend infinitely far along the positive
x_{3}
-axis. The purple boxes have height
\infty
in the positive
x_{4}
-direction. The eight “hidden” boxes at
(i,j,k)
with
0\leq i,j,k\leq 1
are also coloured purple.

x_{3}
x_{2}
x_{1}

Figure 2
.
The solid partition of Example
5.2
.

5.2.
Fixed loci — Local

In this section, we study
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-fixed
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pairs on
\mathbb{C}^{4}
with respect to the standard action, where we do not require the pair to have proper support. These form the building blocks for all further investigations.

\mathrm{DT}
case.
As usual,
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-invariant subschemes of
\mathbb{C}^{4}
correspond to monomial ideals of
\mathbb{C}[x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}]
, which in turn correspond to solid partitions. We denote the subscheme corresponding to a solid partition
\pi
by
Z_{\pi}
.
15
15

15

We use the same correspondence in dimensions 2 and 3 where solid partitions are replaced by partitions and plane partitions respectively.
More precisely, for all
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{4}

x_{1}^{i_{1}}x_{2}^{i_{2}}x_{3}^{i_{3}}x_{4}^{i_{4}}\notin I_{Z_{\pi}}\Leftrightarrow(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\pi.

Let
Z\subset\mathbb{C}^{4}
be a
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-invariant closed subscheme of dimension
\leq 2
. Consider the torsion filtration

T_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\subset T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\subset\mathcal{O}_{Z}.

We have corresponding schemes

Z\supset Z_{0}\supset Z_{1},

where
Z_{1}
has no embedded components of dimension
\leq 1
, i.e.
\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}}
is pure, and
Z_{0}
has no embedded components of dimension 0.

Example 5.3
.

This is a continuation of Example
5.2
. Using the notation of that example, we place an embedded curve at weights
(2,0,i,0)
,
i\geq 2
and we place three more embedded boxes (all with height 0 in direction
x_{4}
). We can view this as stacking boxes
on top
of the solid partition of Example
5.2
with gravity pulling into direction
(-1,-1,-1,-1)
(Figure
3
). In this example, all inclusions of
\varnothing\subset Z_{0}\subset Z_{1}\subset Z
are strict.

x_{3}
x_{2}
x_{1}

Figure 3
.
The solid partition of Example
5.3
.

The subscheme
Z_{1}
is described by six finite partitions

\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\{\lambda_{ab}\}_{1\leq a<b\leq 4}.

More precisely, a monomial
x_{1}^{i_{1}}x_{2}^{i_{2}}x_{3}^{i_{3}}x_{4}^{i_{4}}
lies in
\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}}
if and only if

i_{a},i_{b}\geq 0,\quad\textrm{and }(i_{c},i_{d})\in\lambda_{ab},\quad\exists a,b,c,d\in\{1,2,3,4\}\textrm{ \, distinct \, }a<b,c<d.

Next we consider the scheme
Z_{0}
. Since it has no embedded points, it is determined by the restrictions

Z_{0}|_{\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{3}},\quad Z_{0}|_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}},\quad Z_{0}|_{\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}},\quad Z_{0}|_{\mathbb{C}^{3}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}.

There are corresponding
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{3}
-invariant subschemes
W_{1}
,
W_{2}
,
W_{3}
,
W_{4}\subset\mathbb{C}^{3}
such that

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{Z_{0}|_{\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{3}}}=\mathcal{O}_{W_{1}}[x_{1},x_{1}^{-1}],\quad\mathcal{O}_{Z_{0}|_{\mathbb{C}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}}}=\mathcal{O}_{W_{2}}[x_{2},x_{2}^{-1}],

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{Z_{0}|_{\mathbb{C}^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}}}=\mathcal{O}_{W_{3}}[x_{3},x_{3}^{-1}],\quad\mathcal{O}_{Z_{0}|_{\mathbb{C}^{3}\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}}=\mathcal{O}_{W_{4}}[x_{4},x_{4}^{-1}].

As in
[
MNOP
]
, the subschemes
W_{1},W_{2},W_{3},W_{4}
have dimension
\leq 1
and correspond to plane partitions

\boldsymbol{\mu}=\{\mu_{a}\}_{a=1}^{4}.

The plane partitions
\boldsymbol{\mu}
are of course compatible with the finite partitions
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
. Specifically

(27)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall j\gg 0:\quad(j,k,l)\in\mu_{1}\Leftrightarrow(k,l)\in\lambda_{12},\\
\forall k\gg 0:\quad(j,k,l)\in\mu_{1}\Leftrightarrow(j,l)\in\lambda_{13},\\
\forall l\gg 0:\quad(j,k,l)\in\mu_{1}\Leftrightarrow(j,k)\in\lambda_{14},\end{split}

and similarly for
\mu_{2},\mu_{3},\mu_{4}
. Note that
\mu_{a}
has infinite size unless
\lambda_{ab}=\varnothing
for all
b\neq a
(Definition
5.1
).

We summarize our discussion as follows:

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
Z\subset\mathbb{C}^{4}
be a 2-dimensional
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-invariant closed subscheme. Let
Z\supset Z_{0}\supset Z_{1}
be the subschemes corresponding to the torsion filtration
T_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\subset T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\subset\mathcal{O}_{Z}
. Then

•

Z
is uniquely determined by a solid partition
\pi
,

•

Z_{1}
is uniquely determined by finite partitions
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\{\lambda_{ab}\}_{1\leq a<b\leq 4}
,

•

Z_{0}
is uniquely determined by plane partitions
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\{\mu_{a}\}_{a=1}^{4}
which are compatible with finite partitions
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\{\lambda_{ab}\}_{1\leq a<b\leq 4}
.

This allows us to make the following definition.

Definition 5.5
.

Any collection of finite partitions
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\{\lambda_{ab}\}_{1\leq a<b\leq 4}
(not all empty), determines a unique corresponding
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-invariant pure 2-dimensional subscheme which we denote by
Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{4}
.

If
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\{\mu_{a}\}_{a=1}^{4}
is a choice of plane partitions (not all finite), each corresponding to a subscheme of dimension
\leq 1
in
\mathbb{C}^{3}
, then it uniquely determines a
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-invariant 2-dimensional subscheme without embedded 0-dimensional components, which we denote by
Z_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{4}
.

It is often convenient to represent the partitions in Lemma
5.4
by formal power series in
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4}
. Suppose
Z\subset\mathbb{C}^{4}
is a 2-dimensional
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-invariant closed subschheme with corresponding solid partition
\pi
. Then we write

\pi=\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\geq 0}\sum_{i_{4}=0}^{\pi(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3})}t_{1}^{i_{1}}t_{2}^{i_{2}}t_{3}^{i_{3}}t_{4}^{i_{4}}.

Denoting the partitions corresponding to
Z_{0},Z_{1}
by
\boldsymbol{\mu}
,
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
respectively, we write

\displaystyle\mu_{1}=\sum_{i_{2},i_{3}\geq 0}\sum_{i_{4}=0}^{\mu_{1}(i_{2},i_{3})}t_{2}^{i_{2}}t_{3}^{i_{3}}t_{4}^{i_{4}},\ldots\quad\lambda_{12}=\sum_{i_{3}\geq 0}\sum_{i_{4}=0}^{\lambda_{12}(i_{3})}t_{3}^{i_{3}}t_{4}^{i_{4}},\ldots

Example 5.6
.

Let
I_{Z}=(x_{2}^{2},x_{2}x_{3},x_{3}^{2},x_{2}x_{4},x_{3}x_{4})
. Then
T_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})=0
and

T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})=x_{2}\mathbb{C}[x_{1}]\oplus x_{3}\mathbb{C}[x_{1}].

In particular,
Z_{1}\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}
. Therefore
\lambda_{14}=1
,
\mu_{1}=t_{2}+t_{3}+\frac{1}{1-t_{4}}
,
\mu_{4}=\frac{1}{1-t_{1}}
, and all other
\lambda_{ab}
,
\mu_{a}
are empty. Here
1/(1-t_{i})
is the formal power series obtained by expanding in ascending powers of
t_{i}
.

\mathrm{PT}_{0}
case.
Before we give a combinatorial description of
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-fixed
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
pairs on
\mathbb{C}^{4}
, we study pure 2-dimensional
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-fixed subschemes
Z\subset\mathbb{C}^{4}
. By Lemma
5.4
,
Z=Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
for a collection of finite partitions
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\{\lambda_{ab}\}_{1\leq a<b\leq 4}
. Clearly, each partition
\lambda_{ab}
also determines a pure 2-dimensional scheme
Z_{\lambda_{ab}}
, with irreducible support, and we have

Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=\bigcup_{1\leq a<b\leq 4}Z_{\lambda_{ab}}.

The following proposition classifies when
Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is Cohen-Macaulay:

Proposition 5.7
.

Consider the scheme-theoretic intersection

W=(Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{23}})\cap(Z_{\lambda_{14}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{24}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{34}}).

There are four cases:

\mathrm{(i)}

W
is empty, then
Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is Cohen-Macaulay.

\mathrm{(ii)}

W
is 0-dimensional, then
Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is not Cohen-Macaulay.

\mathrm{(iii)}

W
is 1-dimensional and impure, then
Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is not Cohen-Macaulay.

\mathrm{(iv)}

W
is 1-dimensional and pure, then
Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is Cohen-Macaulay.

Proof.

We use the following characterization: a pure 2-dimensional subscheme
S
in a smooth quasi-projective 4-fold
X
is Cohen-Macaulay if and only if
\curly Ext^{3}_{X}(\mathcal{O}_{S},\mathcal{O}_{X})=0
. We also note that each
Z_{\lambda_{ab}}
is Cohen-Macaulay (being the base change of an element of
\operatorname{Hilb}^{n}(\mathbb{C}^{2})^{(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}}
). Let
X=\mathbb{C}^{4}
.

For (i), there are two cases. Suppose
Z_{\lambda_{14}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{24}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{34}}=\varnothing
. We want to conclude that
Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{23}}
is Cohen-Macaulay. We first observe that
Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}}
is Cohen-Macaulay. This follows from dualizing the short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}}}\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{12}}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{13}}}\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cap Z_{\lambda_{13}}}\to 0

combined with the facts that
Z_{\lambda_{12}}
,
Z_{\lambda_{13}}
are Cohen-Macaulay and
Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cap Z_{\lambda_{13}}
is pure (so
\curly Ext^{3}_{X}(\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{12}}},\mathcal{O}_{X})=\curly Ext^{3}_{X}(\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{13}}},\mathcal{O}_{X})=\curly Ext^{4}_{X}(\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cap Z_{\lambda_{13}}},\mathcal{O}_{X})=0
). Similarly, dualizing

0\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{23}}}\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{23}}}\to\mathcal{O}_{(Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}})\cap Z_{\lambda_{23}}}\to 0

and using purity of
(Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}})\cap Z_{\lambda_{23}}
, we find that
Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{23}}
is Cohen-Macaulay. The second case is
Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{23}}=\varnothing
. Then one can similarly deduce that
Z_{\lambda_{14}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{24}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{34}}
is Cohen-Macaulay.

For (ii), we have —after suitable relabelling—
Z_{\lambda_{12}},Z_{\lambda_{34}}\neq\varnothing
and
Z_{\lambda_{ab}}=\varnothing
otherwise. Then we have a short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{34}}}\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{12}}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{34}}}\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cap Z_{\lambda_{34}}}\to 0,

which is a
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
pair with non-zero cokernel. By Proposition
2.4
, we deduce that
Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{34}}
is not Cohen-Macaulay.

For (iii) and (iv), we consider the short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{23}}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{14}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{24}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{34}}}\to\mathcal{O}_{W}\to 0,

where
Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{23}}
and
Z_{\lambda_{14}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{24}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{34}}
are pure 2-dimensional. If there exists a 0-dimensional subsheaf
0\neq Q\subset\mathcal{O}_{W}
, we obtain a
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
pair with nonzero cokernel

0\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}}\to F\to Q\to 0

and
Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is not Cohen-Macaulay by Proposition
2.4
. Otherwise

\curly Ext_{X}^{3}(\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{23}}},\mathcal{O}_{X})=\curly Ext_{X}^{3}(\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{14}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{24}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{34}}},\mathcal{O}_{X})=\curly Ext_{X}^{4}(\mathcal{O}_{W},\mathcal{O}_{X})=0,

since
W
is pure 1-dimensional and
Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{23}}
,
Z_{\lambda_{14}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{24}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{34}}
are Cohen-Macaulay.
∎

The subscheme
W
can be easily read off from the data
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=\{\lambda_{ab}\}_{1\leq a<b\leq 4}
. Here are some examples:

Example 5.8
.

(i)

If
Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is set theoretically, but not necessarily scheme theoretically, supported in a hyperplane
Z(x_{i})
, then
Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is Cohen-Macaulay.

(ii)

For
\lambda_{12}=\lambda_{34}=1
and
\lambda_{ab}=\varnothing
otherwise, we have
W=\{\mathrm{pt}\}
and
Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is not Cohen-Macaulay.

(iii)

For
\lambda_{12}=\lambda_{13}=\lambda_{34}=1
and
\lambda_{ab}=\varnothing
otherwise, we see that
W=Z(x_{1},x_{2},x_{4})\cong\mathbb{A}^{1}
is pure. Therefore
Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is Cohen-Macaulay. However, for
\lambda_{12}=1+t_{4}
,
\lambda_{13}=\lambda_{34}=1
and
\lambda_{ab}=\varnothing
otherwise, we have
W=Z(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3}x_{4},x_{4}^{2})
which has an embedded point. Therefore
Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
is not Cohen-Macaulay

.

This is a continuation of Example
5.2
. Similar to Example
5.8
(ii),
W
is 0-dimensional for the scheme of Example
5.2
. It has length 4 and its weight spaces are depicted in Figure
4
, where we recall that blue boxes have height 0 in direction
x_{4}
.

W
is a 1-dimensional scheme with 4 embedded points depicted in Figure

(x_{2}=0)
.

.
The solid partition and scheme
W
of Example
5.10
.

By Proposition
2.3
, the
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-fixed
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
pairs on
\mathbb{C}^{4}
with support
Z
correspond to the finitely generated
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-invariant submodules of

(28)

\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z})/\mathcal{O}_{Z},

where
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathcal{O}_{Z}
denotes the ideal sheaf of the origin. The support
Z
may have embedded components of dimension 1, and we denote by
Z_{1}\subset Z
the underlying pure 2-dimensional subscheme. In particular,
Z_{1}=Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
for some finite partitions
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
(Lemma
5.4
) and we have

Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=\bigcup_{1\leq a<b\leq 4}Z_{\lambda_{ab}}.

The following key proposition helps to determine (
28
) in many cases.

Proposition 5.11
.

Let
Z\subset\mathbb{C}^{4}
be a 2-dimensional
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-fixed subscheme without embedded 0-dimensional components. Let
Z_{1}=Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\subset Z
be the underlying pure subscheme defined by
T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\subset\mathcal{O}_{Z}
. Define the scheme

W=(Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{23}})\cap(Z_{\lambda_{14}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{24}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{34}})

and consider the torsion subsheaf
T_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{W})\subset\mathcal{O}_{W}
. Then the induced sequence

0\to\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z}))\to\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z})\to\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}})\to 0

is exact and there is a
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-equivariant isomorphism

(29)

\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}})/\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}}\cong T_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{W}).

Proof.

We start with (
29
). We first observe that

\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{ab}}})\cong\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{ab}}}

and more generally

\displaystyle\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{23}}})\cong\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{23}}},

\displaystyle\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{14}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{24}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{34}}})\cong\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{14}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{24}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{34}}}.

In particular

\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}})\subset\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{23}}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{14}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{24}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{34}}},

and
\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}})/\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}}
is supported on
W
. More precisely, we have

\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}})/\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}}\cong T_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{W}).

The first part follows by applying
\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},\cdot)
to the short exact sequence

0\to T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\to\mathcal{O}_{Z}\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}}\to 0

and noting that
\lim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z})\to\lim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}})
is surjective.
∎

The point of Proposition
5.11
is that we understand
\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}})/\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}}
. Furthermore, the quotient
\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z}))/T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})
is 1-dimensional and is described similarly to the original work of Pandharipande-Thomas
[
PT2
]
(extended to the 4-fold case in
[
CK2
]
and subsequently studied further in
[
Liu
,
CZ
,
Pia
]
).

in Figure

(right).

Definition 5.15
.

Fix plane partitions
\boldsymbol{\mu}
with corresponding 2-dimensional subscheme
Z=Z_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{4}
(Definition
5.5
) and let
M:=\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z})
, where
\mathfrak{m}\subset\mathcal{O}_{Z}
denotes the ideal sheaf of the origin. We refer to the finitely generated
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-invariant submodules
B\subset M/\mathcal{O}_{Z}
as
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
box configurations
with asymptotics
\boldsymbol{\mu}
. We denote the collection of
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
box configurations with asymptotics
\boldsymbol{\mu}
by
\mathcal{B}^{(0)}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
. We say that

has

\mathrm{PT}_{0}
moduli
when there exists no
B^{\prime}\in\mathcal{B}^{(0)}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
with
B^{\prime}\neq B
and

\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(B_{w})=\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(B^{\prime}_{w}),\quad\forall w\in\mathbb{Z}^{4},

where
B_{w},B^{\prime}_{w}
denote the weight spaces of
B,B^{\prime}
for the character
w\in\mathbb{Z}^{4}
respectively. We say that the choice of partitions
\boldsymbol{\mu}
has no
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
moduli when
\mathcal{B}^{(0)}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
contains no elements with
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
moduli. We refer to
|B|=\sum_{w}\dim_{\mathbb{C}}(B_{w})
as the
size
of
B
.

Remark 5.16
.

\boldsymbol{\mu}

17

Similar to the previous definition,
(M/\mathcal{O}_{Z})_{w}

for the character

.

18

subject to the condition that gravity pulls in direction

.

The following corollary gives many examples of choices of plane partitions with no

Corollary 5.19
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
be finite partitions and let
\boldsymbol{\mu}
be compatible plane partitions, i.e., satisfying (
27
). Let
a,b,c,d\in\{1,2,3,4\}
be mutually distinct, suppose
\mu_{a}
,
\mu_{b}
have no further restrictions, and
\mu_{c}
,
\mu_{d}
are minimal, i.e.,
\mu_{c}=\mu_{c}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
,
\mu_{d}=\mu_{d}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
.
20
20

20

I.e., the scheme
Z_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
has embedded 1-dimensional components in at most two directions.
Then
\boldsymbol{\mu}
has no
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
moduli.

Proof.

We use Proposition
5.11
. Consider the weight spaces

\Big(\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z}))/T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z})\Big)_{w},\quad\Big(\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}})/\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}}\Big)_{w}

These weight spaces are at most 1-dimensional and they are not 1-dimensional for the same
w
.
∎

Example 5.20
.

(i)

In general, when there are
\geq 3
embedded legs, there will be
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
moduli (as in the curve case). For example, let
I_{Z}=(x_{1}x_{2}x_{3},x_{1}x_{3}^{2},x_{2}x_{3}^{2},x_{4})
. This corresponds to

,

,
\mu_{3}=1
, and

otherwise. Then
(\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z})/\mathcal{O}_{Z})_{(0,0,1,0)}=\mathbb{C}^{2}
and there are
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
moduli.

(ii)

However, there are examples with
\geq 3
embedded legs for which there are no
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
moduli such as
I_{Z}=(x_{2}x_{3},x_{1}x_{3}^{2},x_{1}x_{4},x_{3}x_{4},x_{4}^{2})
. This corresponds to
\lambda_{12}=1
,
\mu_{1}=t_{3}+\frac{1}{1-t_{2}}
,

,
\mu_{3}=1
, and
\lambda_{ab}=\mu_{4}=\varnothing
otherwise.

\mathrm{PT}_{1}
case.
By Proposition
2.3
, the
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-fixed
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
pairs on
\mathbb{C}^{4}
with support
Z
correspond to the finitely generated
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-invariant submodules of

\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{I}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z})/\mathcal{O}_{Z},

where
\mathfrak{I}\subset\mathcal{O}_{Z}
denotes the ideal sheaf of the union of coordinate axes contained in
Z
. Recall that
Z=Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
for some finite partitions
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
(Lemma
5.4
) and

Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}=\bigcup_{1\leq a<b\leq 4}Z_{\lambda_{ab}}.

Proposition 5.21
.

Let
Z=Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}\subset\mathbb{C}^{4}
and denote by
\mathfrak{I}\subset\mathcal{O}_{Z}
the ideal sheaf of the union of the coordinate axes contained in
Z
. Then

\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{I}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z})=\bigoplus_{1\leq a<b\leq 4}\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{ab}}}[x_{a}^{-1},x_{b}^{-1}].

Proof.

From the restrictions
\mathcal{O}_{Z}\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{ab}}}
, we obtain a short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{Z}\to\bigoplus_{1\leq a<b\leq 4}\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{ab}}}\to C\to 0,

where the cokernel
C
is supported on the union of coordinate axes contained in
Z
. Then

\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{I}^{r},C)=0

and therefore

\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{I}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z})=\bigoplus_{1\leq a<b\leq 4}\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{I}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{ab}}}).

Finally, we have

\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{I}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{ab}}})\cong\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\lambda_{ab}}}[x_{a}^{-1},x_{b}^{-1}].\qed

Remark 5.24
.

\boldsymbol{\lambda}

,

, then
w\in B
. Moreover,
B
is required to support a
finitely generated
module.

The following is an immediate consequence of Proposition
5.21
.

Corollary 5.26
.

A collection of finite partitions
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
has no
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
moduli if and only if
\lambda_{ab}=\varnothing
unless
(a,b)=(1,2)
or
(3,4)
(up to relabelling).

Remark 5.27
.

By Example
5.8
(iii) it can happen that
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
has
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
moduli but that
\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
(the minimal plane partitions compatible with
\boldsymbol{\lambda}
) has no
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
moduli. This simply means that
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-equivariant
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
pairs on supported on
Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
with 1-dimensional cokernel may have moduli but those with 0-dimensional cokernel do not.

We will see shortly that Corollaries
5.19
and
5.26
together imply that for toric Calabi-Yau 4-folds of the form
X=\mathrm{Tot}(L_{1}\oplus L_{2})\to S
, where
S
is a smooth projective toric surface and
L_{1},L_{2}
line bundles on
S
satisfying
L_{1}\otimes L_{2}\cong K_{S}
, the
T_{X}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
fixed loci of the
\mathrm{DT},\mathrm{PT}_{0},\mathrm{PT}_{1}
moduli spaces are always 0-dimensional (Remark
5.31
).

Example 5.28
.

Consider the solid partition corresponding to
S\cong\mathbb{C}^{2}
defined by the ideal
(x_{3},x_{4})
. Then
\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{I}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{S})/\mathcal{O}_{S}=\mathbb{C}[x_{1}^{\pm 1},x_{2}^{\pm 1}]/\mathbb{C}[x_{1},x_{2}]
. In Figure
10
(left) we depict an example of a
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
box configuration. Since
S
is smooth, by Proposition
2.7
,
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
pairs
(F,s)
with support
S
and non-zero cokernel
Q
correspond to nestings
Z\subset C\subset S
where
Z
is 0-dimensional and
C
is an effective divisor. Furthermore, the cokernel
Q
satisfies
Q\cong\jmath_{*}I_{Z/C}(C)
, and
(F,s)
is
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-equivariant if and only if
Z\subset C\subset S
is a
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}
-invariant nesting. In Figure
10
(right), we have drawn in red the weight spaces corresponding to
I_{Z/C}(C)\subset\mathcal{O}_{C}(C)
and in green the weight spaces corresponding to
\mathcal{O}_{Z}(C)
.

x_{3}
x_{2}
x_{1}

x_{3}
x_{2}
x_{1}

.
