pair of Example
5.13
.

5.3.
Fixed loci — Global

We turn our attention to the characterization of
T_{X}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
fixed
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
stable pairs on an arbitrary smooth quasi-projective toric 4-fold
X
. Given a
T_{X}
-fixed
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
stable pair
(F,s)
on
X
, we can consider the restrictions

(F^{(\alpha)},s^{(\alpha)}):=(F,s)|_{U_{\alpha}},\quad\alpha\in V(X).

Since
U_{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{C}^{4}
, we can use the previous section to describe
(F^{(\alpha)},s^{(\alpha)})
combinatorially. Denote the scheme theoretic support of
(F,s)
by
Z
and recall the closed subschemes

Z\supset Z_{0}\supset Z_{1}

defined by the torsion filtration of
\mathcal{O}_{Z}
. Recall that for
q=0
, we have
Z=Z_{0}
and for
q=1
we have
Z=Z_{1}
. We define
Z^{(\alpha)}:=Z\cap U_{\alpha}
,
Z^{(\alpha)}_{0}:=Z_{0}\cap U_{\alpha}
, and
Z^{(\alpha)}_{1}:=Z_{1}\cap U_{\alpha}
. Then there exist collections of finite partitions and compatible (
27
) plane partitions

\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}=\{\lambda_{ab}^{(\alpha)}\}_{1\leq a<b\leq 4\atop\alpha\in V(X)},\quad\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(\alpha)}=\{\mu_{a}^{(\alpha)}\}_{1\leq a\leq 4\atop\alpha\in V(X)}

such that (Definition
5.5
)

Z_{0}^{(\alpha)}=Z_{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(\alpha)}},\quad Z_{1}^{(\alpha)}=Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}}.

The partitions
\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(\alpha)},\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(\alpha)}
satisfy gluing conditions. For two charts
U_{\alpha},U_{\beta}
intersecting in
\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{3}
, let us label the coordinates in each chart such that the first coordinate corresponds to
\mathbb{C}^{*}
. Then

(31)

\mu_{1}^{(\alpha)}=\mu_{1}^{(\beta)},\quad\lambda^{(\alpha)}_{1b}=\lambda^{(\beta)}_{1b},\quad\forall b=2,3,4.

By the compatibility (
27
), the equations on plane partitions imply the ones on partitions.
For two charts
U_{\alpha},U_{\beta}
intersecting in
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
, let us label the coordinates in each chart such that the first two coordinates correspond to
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}
. Then

(32)

\lambda^{(\alpha)}_{12}=\lambda^{(\beta)}_{12}.

For
q\in\{-1,0\}
, the gluing conditions (
31
), (
32
) completely determine when a collection of
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-equivariant
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pairs
\{(F^{(\alpha)},s^{(\alpha)})\}_{\alpha\in V(X)}
on the charts
U_{\alpha}
glues to a
T_{X}
-equivariant
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
pair on
X
. For
q=1
, one additionally requires that the
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
box configurations glue.

In Section
2.2
, we discussed the moduli spaces

(33)

{\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X),\quad q\in\{-1,0,1\},\quad v=(0,0,\gamma,\beta,n-\gamma\cdot\mathrm{td}_{2}(X))\in H_{c}^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q}).

Let
X
be a toric Calabi-Yau 4-fold and denote the (3-dimensional) subtorus preserving the Calabi-Yau volume form by
T\leq T_{X}
. The following theorem provides a nice collection of cases where Oh-Thomas localization for 0-dimensional reduced
T
-fixed loci as in Proposition
2.13
applies.

Theorem 5.29
.

Let
\curly P:={\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
.

\mathrm{(1)}

For
q=-1
, we have
\curly P^{T_{X}}=\curly P^{T}
and it is 0-dimensional and reduced.

\mathrm{(2)}

For
q=0
and
\mathrm{PT}_{0}=\mathrm{PT}_{1}
, i.e.
F
is pure for all
(F,s)\in\curly P^{T_{X}}
, we have
\curly P^{T_{X}}=\curly P^{T}
and it is 0-dimensional and reduced.

\mathrm{(3)}

For
q=0
, suppose for any
(F,s)\in\curly P^{T_{X}}
and any
\alpha\in V(X)
the scheme theoretic support of
F|_{U_{\alpha}}
has at most two embedded 1-dimensional components and its pure part is Cohen-Macaulay. Then
\curly P^{T_{X}}=\curly P^{T}
and it is 0-dimensional and reduced.

Proof.

\mathrm{DT}
case.
Clearly, we have

\curly P_{v}^{(-1)}(X)^{T_{X}}\subset\curly P_{v}^{(-1)}(X)^{T}.

By comparing
T
-fixed ideals and
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-fixed ideals on each chart
U_{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{C}^{4}
, it is easy to see that this is an equality at the level of closed points. The argument is written out in
[
CK1
, Lem. 3.1]
for
\gamma=\beta=0
, but it works for 2-dimensional subschemes as well. Next, we claim that for any
Z\in\curly P_{v}^{(-1)}(X)^{T_{X}}
, we have
\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I_{Z},\mathcal{O}_{Z})^{T}=0
, which establishes (1).

In fact, we claim the stronger statement

\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\alpha}}(I_{Z_{\alpha}},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}})^{T}=0

on each
U_{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{C}^{4}
. Since
I_{Z_{\alpha}}
is a monomial ideal,
\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\alpha}}(I_{Z_{\alpha}},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}})
has an explicit combinatorial basis in terms of so-called Haiman arrows
[
MS
]
. From this description, it is clear that there are no non-zero
T
-fixed Haiman arrows. This is worked out in
[
CK1
, Lem. 3.6]
for
\gamma=\beta=0
, but the argument holds for 2-dimensional subschemes as well (but it is crucial that
Z_{\alpha}
has codimension
\geq 2
).

\mathrm{PT}_{0}
case.

Since the fixed locus
\curly P^{T}
is proper and
\curly P^{T_{X}}\subset\curly P^{T}
, in order to show that
\curly P^{T}=\curly P^{T_{X}}
is 0-dimensional reduced, it suffices to show that
\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},F)^{T}=0
for any
I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}=[\mathcal{O}_{X}\to F]\in\curly P^{T_{X}}
.

We start by following
[
PT2
, Sect. 3.1, 3.2]
. Take
I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}=[\mathcal{O}_{X}\to F]\in\curly P^{T_{X}}
, denote its cokernel by
Q
, and its scheme theoretic support by
Z
. We write
I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}_{\alpha}:=I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}|_{U_{\alpha}}
,
F_{\alpha}:=F|_{U_{\alpha}}
,
Q_{\alpha}:=Q|_{U_{\alpha}}
, and
Z_{\alpha}=Z|_{U_{\alpha}}
. Consider the restriction morphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},F)\to\bigoplus_{\alpha\in V(X)}\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\alpha}}(I_{\alpha}^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},F_{\alpha}).

From the exact triangle
I_{Z_{\alpha}}\to I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}_{\alpha}\to Q_{\alpha}[-1]
we obtain the left exact sequence

0\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(Q_{\alpha},F_{\alpha})\to\operatorname{Hom}(I_{\alpha}^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},F_{\alpha})\to\operatorname{Hom}(I_{Z_{\alpha}},F_{\alpha}),

which remains left exact after taking
T
-fixed part. Suppose
\operatorname{Hom}(I_{Z_{\alpha}},F_{\alpha})^{T}=0
for all
\alpha\in V(X)
. Then
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(Q_{\alpha},F_{\alpha})^{T}\cong\operatorname{Hom}(I_{\alpha}^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},F_{\alpha})^{T}
. Moreover, since
Q_{\alpha}
is 0-dimensional, we deduce (by
[
PT2
]
paragraph below equation (3.2)) that the restriction morphism induces an isomorphism

\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},F)^{T}\cong\bigoplus_{\alpha\in V(X)}\operatorname{Hom}_{U_{\alpha}}(I_{\alpha}^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},F_{\alpha})^{T}.

Next, we define
M_{\alpha}:=\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}})
, where
\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}\subset\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}}
is the ideal sheaf of the origin. In fact, it is easier to work with a sufficient approximation
M_{\alpha}^{r}:=\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}^{r},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}})
, with
r\gg 0
such that
F_{\alpha}\subset M_{\alpha}^{r}
(so
M_{\alpha}^{r}
is coherent).
We then obtain an exact sequence

\operatorname{Hom}(Q_{\alpha},M_{\alpha}^{r})\to\operatorname{Hom}(Q_{\alpha},M_{\alpha}^{r}/F_{\alpha})\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(Q_{\alpha},F_{\alpha})\to\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(Q_{\alpha},M_{\alpha}^{r}).

Summarizing: in order to prove that
\curly P^{T}
is 0-dimensional reduced, it suffices to show that for any
I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}=[\mathcal{O}_{X}\to F]\in\curly P^{T_{X}}
we have
\operatorname{Hom}(I_{Z_{\alpha}},F_{\alpha})^{T}=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(Q_{\alpha},F_{\alpha})^{T}=0
for all
\alpha\in V(X)
. In turn, for this it suffices to show

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(I_{Z_{\alpha}},F_{\alpha})^{T}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(Q_{\alpha},M^{r}_{\alpha})^{T}

\displaystyle=0,

\displaystyle\operatorname{Hom}(Q_{\alpha},M_{\alpha}^{r}/F_{\alpha})^{T}

\displaystyle=0,

for all
I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}=[\mathcal{O}_{X}\to F]\in\curly P^{T_{X}}
and
\alpha\in V(X)
.

We now prove (2). We first study
\operatorname{Hom}(I_{Z_{\alpha}},F_{\alpha})^{T}
. Since we assume
\mathrm{PT}_{0}=\mathrm{PT}_{1}
,
Z_{\alpha}
is pure 2-dimensional.
We first observe that, by the
\mathrm{DT}
case, we have
\operatorname{Hom}(I_{Z_{\alpha}},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}})^{T}=0
. We now consider
\operatorname{Hom}(I_{Z_{\alpha}},Q_{\alpha})^{T}
. Take a homogeneous element of
\operatorname{Hom}(I_{Z_{\alpha}},Q_{\alpha})^{T}
. By Proposition
5.11
, it will map a homogeneous element
g
of
I_{Z_{\alpha}}
to a weight space contained in
T_{0}(\mathcal{O}_{W})
. Then
g
maps to zero by an argument similar to the
\mathrm{DT}
case.

It suffices to show that
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(Q_{\alpha},F_{\alpha})^{T}=0
. Writing
Z_{\alpha}=Z_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}
, we set
S_{1}:=Z_{\lambda_{12}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{13}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{23}}
and
S_{2}:=Z_{\lambda_{14}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{24}}\cup Z_{\lambda_{34}}
. Then Corollary
5.14
gives a short exact sequence

\cdots\rightarrow\operatorname{Hom}(Q_{\alpha},\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}\cap S_{2}}/Q_{\alpha})\rightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(Q_{\alpha},F_{\alpha})\rightarrow\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(Q_{\alpha},\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{S_{2}})\rightarrow\cdots,

where the scheme theoretic support of
Q_{\alpha}
is contained in
S_{1}\cap S_{2}
.
Since
Q_{\alpha}
is 0-dimensional and
\mathcal{O}_{S_{i}}
is pure 2-dimensional and Cohen-Macaulay (Proposition
5.7
), we have
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(Q_{\alpha},\mathcal{O}_{S_{i}})=0
. Indeed, since
Q_{\alpha}
is 0-dimensional
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(Q_{\alpha},\mathcal{O}_{S_{i}})
is finite-dimensional and dual to
\operatorname{Ext}^{3}(\mathcal{O}_{S_{i}},Q_{\alpha})
. At the stalk at 0, the module
\mathcal{O}_{S_{i},0}
has depth 2 (Cohen-Macaulay property) and hence projective dimension 2 (Auslander-Buchsbaum), so

\operatorname{Ext}_{\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}^{4},0}}^{3}(\mathcal{O}_{S_{i},0},Q_{\alpha,0})=0.

Furthermore, similar to the
\mathrm{DT}
case, one can show

\operatorname{Hom}(Q_{\alpha},\mathcal{O}_{S_{1}\cap S_{2}}/Q_{\alpha})^{T}=0.

Next, we prove (3). By assumption, the support of
T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}})
has at most two irreducible components for all
\alpha\in V(X)
. We first study
\operatorname{Hom}(I_{Z_{\alpha}},F_{\alpha})^{T}
and note that, by the
\mathrm{DT}
case,
\operatorname{Hom}(I_{Z_{\alpha}},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}})^{T}=0
. We consider
\operatorname{Hom}(I_{Z_{\alpha}},Q_{\alpha})^{T}
. Take a homogeneous element of
\operatorname{Hom}(I_{Z_{\alpha}},Q_{\alpha})^{T}
. It maps a homogeneous element
g
of
I_{Z_{\alpha}}
to a weight space of
Q_{\alpha}
supported on

\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r}_{\alpha},T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}}))/T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}})

by Proposition
5.11
. It can then be seen that
g
maps to zero by a reasoning similar to
[
PT2
, Lem. 2]
. Thus
\operatorname{Hom}(I_{Z_{\alpha}},F_{\alpha})^{T}=0
.

By assumption, the pure part
Z_{1}\subset Z_{\alpha}
is Cohen-Macaulay. Propositions
5.11
,
5.7
give a short exact sequence

0\to\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}_{\alpha}^{r},T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}}))\to M_{\alpha}\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}}\to 0.

Furthermore, by assumption, the support of
T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}})
has at most two irreducible components. In particular,
(F_{\alpha},s_{\alpha})
has no
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
moduli (Corollary
5.19
). Without loss of generality, we take the reduced support of
T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}})
to be contained in the union of the
x_{1}
- and
x_{2}
-axis. We first show
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(Q_{\alpha},M^{r}_{\alpha})^{T}=0
.
There exists a
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{3}
-equivariant 0-dimensional
\mathbb{C}[x_{2},x_{3},x_{4}]
-module
N_{1}
and
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{3}
-equivariant 0-dimensional
\mathbb{C}[x_{1},x_{3},x_{4}]
-module
N_{2}
such that

\varinjlim\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r}_{\alpha},T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}}))=M_{1}\oplus M_{2},\quad M_{i}=N_{i}[x_{i},x_{i}^{-1}],\quad i=1,2.

Using the truncated versions
M_{\alpha}^{r},M_{1}^{r},M_{2}^{r}
of
M_{\alpha},M_{1},M_{2}
, we have a short exact sequence

(34)

0\to M_{1}^{r}\oplus M_{2}^{r}\to M_{\alpha}^{r}\to\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}}\to 0.

Note that

M_{i}^{r+N}=M_{i}^{r}\otimes t_{i}^{-N},

for all
N>0
. Since
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(Q_{\alpha},M_{i}^{r})
are finite
T
-representations (
Q_{\alpha}
is 0-dimensional), we can take
N
sufficiently large such that

\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(Q_{\alpha},M_{i}^{r+N})=\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(Q_{\alpha},M_{i}^{r})\otimes t_{i}^{-N}

has no
T
-fixed part (this trick is also used in
[
PT2
, Lem. 1]
). Furthermore, since
Q_{\alpha}
is 0-dimensional and
Z_{1}
is 2-dimensional Cohen-Macaulay, we have
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(Q_{\alpha},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{1}})=0
(as we saw above). Consequently, for
r\gg 0
, (
34
) implies
\operatorname{Ext}^{1}(Q_{\alpha},M^{r}_{\alpha})^{T}=0
.

Finally, we show that
\operatorname{Hom}(Q_{\alpha},M_{\alpha}^{r}/F_{\alpha})^{T}=0
. Consider

\operatorname{Hom}(Q_{\alpha},M_{\alpha}^{r}/F_{\alpha})\cong\operatorname{Hom}(Q_{\alpha},(\mathcal{H}{\it{om}}(\mathfrak{m}^{r}_{\alpha},T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}}))\cap F_{\alpha})/T_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}})).

The vanishing of the
T
-fixed part on the right hand side follows by an argument which is almost the same as
[
CK2
, Prop. 2.6, Step 3]
.
∎

We expect that there are many more cases where
\curly P^{T_{X}}=\curly P^{T}
is 0-dimensional and reduced, but the combinatorics involved in the tangent space analysis becomes increasingly complicated:

Conjecture 5.30
.

Let
X
be a toric Calabi-Yau 4-fold and
\curly P:={\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
for
q\in\{-1,0,1\}
. If
\curly P^{T_{X}}
is 0-dimensional, then
\curly P^{T_{X}}=\curly P^{T}
and it is reduced.

Evidence for the conjecture is provided by the cases proved in Theorem
5.29
. Further indications that this conjecture is true come from the fact that in all examples we calculated where
\curly P^{T_{X}}
is 0-dimensional (including, e.g., for
q=1
the examples in Section
6.6
), we never found a
T
-fixed term in
T_{\curly P}^{\mathrm{vir}}|_{I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}}
. This does not imply that
\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},F)^{T}
is zero, because it could cancel against a
T
-fixed term of the same dimension in the obstructions. See also
[
PT2
, Sect. 3.3]
for an analogous result in the 3-fold case.

Remark 5.31
.

Suppose
X=\mathrm{Tot}(L_{1}\oplus L_{2})\to S
, where
S
is a smooth projective toric surface and
L_{1},L_{2}
line bundles on
S
satisfying
L_{1}\otimes L_{2}\cong K_{S}
. Let
\curly P:={\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
for
q\in\{-1,0,1\}
and
v=(0,0,\gamma,\beta,n-\gamma\cdot\mathrm{td}_{2}(X))\in H_{c}^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})
. By Corollaries
5.19
,
5.26
, and the gluing description at the beginning of this section, it is clear that
\curly P^{T_{X}}
is always 0-dimensional. For
q\in\{-1,0\}
, we have
\curly P^{T_{X}}=\curly P^{T}
is reduced by Theorem
5.29
(1) and (3). For
q=1
and assuming Conjecture
5.30
, we have
\curly P^{T_{X}}=\curly P^{T}
is reduced.

6.
Vertex formalism

In this section, we investigate the calculation of
K
-theoretic
\mathrm{PT}_{q}
invariants of toric Calabi-Yau 4-folds for
q\in\{-1,0,1\}
. We also formulate a
K
-theoretic
\mathrm{DT}
–
\mathrm{PT}_{0}
vertex correspondence, which we verify in some examples.

6.1.
Redistribution

Let
X
be a toric Calabi-Yau 4-fold. Let
\curly P:={\curly P}^{(q)}_{v}(X)
with
q\in\{-1,0,1\}
with
v
as in (
33
). We now present a method to determine the virtual tangent representation
21
21

21

Under this isomorphism,
[\mathcal{O}_{\mathrm{pt}}]
corresponds to
1
.

R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet},I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet})_{0}[1]\in K_{0}^{T_{X}}(\mathrm{pt})\cong\mathbb{Z}[t_{1}^{\pm 1},t_{2}^{\pm 1},t_{3}^{\pm 1},t_{4}^{\pm 1}],

for any
T_{X}
-fixed
I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}\in\curly P
. Note that the spaces
\operatorname{Ext}_{X}^{i}(I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}},I^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}})_{0}
are finite-dimensional (Section
2.2
). We observe that the rank of this complex is even because
N_{S_{f}/X}
is split for all
f\in F(X)
(Lemma
2.11
). There exists a smooth projective toric 4-fold
\overline{X}
and a
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-equivariant good compactification
j:X\hookrightarrow\overline{X}
. We then have

\mathrm{vd}:=n-\frac{1}{2}(j_{*}\gamma)^{2}\in\mathbb{Z}.

We write out the case
q=-1
and comment on the differences for
q\in\{0,1\}
in Remarks
6.14
,
6.15
.
Then
I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}=I_{Z}
for some
T_{X}
-fixed subscheme
Z\subset X
. The
q=-1
was recently also developed by Nekrasov-Piazzalunga
[
NP2
]
(see Section
1.5
).

Consider the open affine invariant cover
\{U_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha\in V(X)}
of
X
. We write
U_{\alpha\beta}=U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}
,
U_{\alpha\beta\gamma}=U_{\alpha}\cap U_{\beta}\cap U_{\gamma}
, etc. and we choose a total order on the indices
\alpha
. We also write
Z_{\alpha}=Z\cap U_{\alpha}
,
I_{\alpha}=I_{Z_{\alpha}}
,
Z_{\alpha\beta}=Z\cap U_{\alpha\beta}
,
I_{\alpha\beta}=I_{Z_{\alpha\beta}}
,
Z_{\alpha\beta\gamma}=Z\cap U_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
,
I_{\alpha\beta\gamma}=I_{Z_{\alpha\beta\gamma}}
etc. Using the local-to-global spectral sequence and Čech cohomology, as in
[
MNOP
, Sect. 4.6]
, we obtain

\displaystyle R\mathrm{Hom}_{X}(I_{Z},I_{Z})_{0}[1]=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in V(X)}R\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{\alpha}}(I_{\alpha},I_{\alpha})_{0}[1]-\sum_{\alpha<\beta\in V(X)}R\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{\alpha\beta}}(I_{\alpha\beta},I_{\alpha\beta})_{0}[1]

\displaystyle+\sum_{\alpha<\beta<\gamma\in V(X)}R\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{\alpha\beta\gamma}}(I_{\alpha\beta\gamma},I_{\alpha\beta\gamma})_{0}[1]-\cdots.

We always label edges
\alpha\beta\in E(X)
so that
\alpha<\beta
. The previous equation should be read as an identity in the additive group
\mathbb{Q}(\!(t_{1},t_{1}^{-1},\ldots,t_{4},t_{4}^{-1})\!)
: the individual summands on the right hand side are generally not finite but, when adding them, there are infinitely many cancellations resulting in an element in
\mathbb{Q}[t_{1},t_{1}^{-1},\ldots,t_{4},t_{4}^{-1}]\subset\mathbb{Q}(\!(t_{1},t_{1}^{-1},\ldots,t_{4},t_{4}^{-1})\!)
.

Remark 6.1
.

Since
Z
is
T_{X}
-invariant, 2-dimensional, and proper, the only contributions in the above sum are:

•

\alpha\in V(X)
, which corresponds to
U_{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{C}^{4}
,

•

\alpha<\beta\in V(X)
such that
\alpha\beta\in E(X)
, which corresponds to
U_{\alpha\beta}\cong\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{3}
,

•

\alpha<\beta\in V(X)
such that
\alpha\beta\notin E(X)
but
\alpha,\beta\in f
for some
f\in F(X)
, which corresponds to
U_{\alpha\beta}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
,

•

\alpha_{1}<\cdots<\alpha_{N}\in V(X)
with
N\geq 3
such that
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{N}\in f
for some
f\in F(X)
, which corresponds to
U_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{N}}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
.

In order to deduce these statements, it is useful to recall the description of fans of toric Calabi-Yau 4-folds. Let
N=\mathbb{Z}^{4}
and
N_{\mathbb{R}}=N\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R}
. Consider the hyperplane
\{x_{4}=1\}\subset N_{\mathbb{R}}
and any collection of (not necessarily regular) rational tetrahedra in
\{x_{4}=1\}
such that any two are disjoint or intersect in a common vertex, edge, or facet. Then we can consider the induced fan
\Delta
of strongly convex rational polyhedral cones in
N_{\mathbb{R}}
spanned by the origin and the tetrahedra in
\{x_{4}=1\}
. Fans obtained in this way are precisely the fans of toric Calabi-Yau 4-folds.

By Lemma
5.4
,
I_{\alpha}\subset\mathcal{O}_{U_{\alpha}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{C}^{4}}
corresponds to a solid partition
\pi^{(\alpha)}\subset\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}^{4}
with asymptotic finite partitions and (compatible) plane partitions

\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}=\{\lambda_{ab}^{(\alpha)}\}_{1\leq a<b\leq 4},\quad\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(\alpha)}=\{\mu_{a}^{(\alpha)}\}_{a=1}^{4}.

Suppose we choose coordinate functions
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
on
U_{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{C}^{4}
such that
t:=(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4})\in T_{X}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
acts by

(35)

t\cdot(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})=(t_{1}x_{1},t_{2}x_{2},t_{3}x_{3},t_{4}x_{4}).

Then we denote the
T_{X}
-character of
\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}}
by
22
22

22

For any
M\in K_{0}^{(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}}(\mathbb{C}^{4})
, we denote by
\operatorname{tr}_{M}
the virtual
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-representation of
M
. It is viewed as a rational function in
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4}
and the dimensions of the weight spaces of
M
are obtained by expanding this rational function in
ascending
powers of
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4}
. For example, for
M=\mathcal{O}_{Z}
and
Z=Z(x_{1},x_{2})\cup Z(x_{3},x_{4})
, we have
\operatorname{tr}_{\mathcal{O}_{Z}}=(1-t_{1})^{-1}(1-t_{2})^{-1}+(1-t_{3})^{-1}(1-t_{4})^{-1}-1
.

(36)

Z_{\alpha}:=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}}}=\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})\in\pi^{(\alpha)}}t_{1}^{i_{1}}t_{2}^{i_{2}}t_{3}^{i_{3}}t_{4}^{i_{4}}.

Let
\alpha\beta\in E(X)
. Then
U_{\alpha\beta}\cong\mathbb{C}^{*}\times\mathbb{C}^{3}
, and we label coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
such that
x_{1}
corresponds to the first
\mathbb{C}^{*}
factor (and
T_{X}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
acts by the standard torus action (
35
)).
Let
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{N}\in V(X)
such that
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{N}\in f
for some
f\in F(X)
, where for
N=2
, we assume
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\not\in E(X)
.
Then
U_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{N}}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
(Remark
6.1
).
We label coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
on
U_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{N}}
such that
(x_{1},x_{2})
corresponds to the
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}
factor (and
T_{X}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
acts by the standard torus action (
35
)).
Similar to (
36
), we define

Z_{\alpha\beta}:=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha\beta}}},\quad Z_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{N}}:=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{N}}}}.

Using the Taylor resolution
[
Tay
]

0\to F_{s}\to\cdots\to F_{1}\to F_{0}\to I_{\alpha}\to 0,

a calculation similar to
[
MNOP
, Sect. 4.7]
gives

(37)

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{R\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{\alpha}}(I_{\alpha},I_{\alpha})_{0}[1]}=Z_{\alpha}+\frac{\overline{Z}_{\alpha}}{t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}t_{4}}-\frac{P_{1234}}{t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}t_{4}}Z_{\alpha}\overline{Z}_{\alpha},

where
\overline{(\cdot)}
denotes the dual representation, which amounts to replacing each
t_{i}
by
t_{i}^{-1}
, and

\displaystyle P_{1234}:=(1-t_{1})(1-t_{2})(1-t_{3})(1-t_{4}).

The rational function (
37
) in
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4}
should be expanded in
ascending
powers of
t_{i}
.

Furthermore, we have

(38)

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{R\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{\alpha\beta}}(I_{\alpha\beta},I_{\alpha\beta})_{0}[1]}=\delta(t_{1})\Bigg(Z_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{\overline{Z}_{\alpha\beta}}{t_{2}t_{3}t_{4}}+\frac{P_{234}}{t_{2}t_{3}t_{4}}Z_{\alpha\beta}\overline{Z}_{\alpha\beta}\Bigg),

where

\displaystyle P_{ijk}:=(1-t_{i})(1-t_{j})(1-t_{k}),\quad\delta(t_{i}):=\sum_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}t_{i}.

The rational function (
38
) in
t_{2},t_{3},t_{4}
between brackets should be expanded in
ascending
powers of
t_{2},t_{3},t_{4}
.

Finally, we have

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{R\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{N}}}(I_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{N}},I_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{N}})_{0}[1]}=\delta(t_{1})\delta(t_{2})\Bigg(Z_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{N}}+\frac{\overline{Z}_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{N}}}{t_{3}t_{4}}-\frac{P_{34}}{t_{3}t_{4}}Z_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{N}}\overline{Z}_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{N}}\Bigg),

where

\displaystyle\quad P_{ij}:=(1-t_{i})(1-t_{j}).

Note that

Z_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{N}}+\frac{\overline{Z}_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{N}}}{t_{3}t_{4}}-\frac{P_{34}}{t_{3}t_{4}}Z_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{N}}\overline{Z}_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{N}}

is a Laurent
polynomial
in
t_{3},t_{4}
. However, (
37
) and the term between brackets in (
38
) are in general infinite Laurent
series
(in ascending powers of the
t_{i}
). We therefore redistribute the terms.
This means we want to attach Laurent polynomials to all elements of
V(X)
,
E(X)
,
F(X)
which sum up to the
T_{X}
-representation
R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I_{Z},I_{Z})_{0}[1]
.

In this section, we denote the vertices neighbouring a vertex
\alpha
by
\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3},\beta_{4}
.
23
23

23

Since
X
is non-compact,
\alpha
may lie on a non-compact edge in which case there are
<4
neighbouring vertices.
Then we label the coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
on
U_{\alpha}\cong\mathbb{C}^{4}
such that

L_{\alpha\beta_{i}}=Z(x_{j},x_{k},x_{l}),

for all
i,j,k,l\in\{1,2,3,4\}
distinct (and
T_{X}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
acts by the standard torus action (
35
)).

In the following four definitions, we fix
Z\subset X
a proper 2-dimensional
T_{X}
-fixed subscheme. Recall that, for any
\alpha
,
Z_{\alpha}
determines partitions in various dimensions (Lemma
5.4
)

\{\pi^{(\alpha)}\}_{\alpha\in V(X)},\quad\{\boldsymbol{\mu}^{(\alpha)}\}_{\alpha\in V(X)},\quad\{\boldsymbol{\lambda}^{(\alpha)}\}_{\alpha\in V(X)}.

Definition 6.2
.

By the inclusion-exclusion principle, there exist unique Laurent
polynomials

W_{\alpha\beta_{i}}
,
W_{\alpha}
such that
W_{\alpha\beta_{i}}
does not depend on
t_{i}
and

\displaystyle Z_{\alpha}=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 4}\frac{Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{j}}}{(1-t_{i})(1-t_{j})}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\frac{W_{\alpha\beta_{i}}}{1-t_{i}}+W_{\alpha}.

Recall that
Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{j}}
are also Laurent polynomials. Then
|Z_{\alpha}|:=\operatorname{rk}(W_{\alpha})
is called the
renormalized volume
of
Z_{\alpha}
. We also write

|\pi^{(\alpha)}|:=\operatorname{rk}(W_{\alpha}).

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}}=\sum_{a\in\{j,k,l\}}\frac{Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{a}}}{1-t_{a}}+W_{\alpha\beta_{i}}.

Then
|Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}}|:=\operatorname{rk}(W_{\alpha\beta_{i}})
is called the renormalized volume of
Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}}
. Moreover

|\mu_{i}^{(\alpha)}|:=\operatorname{rk}(W_{\alpha\beta_{i}})

coincides with the renormalized volume of plane partitions defined in
[
MNOP
, Sect. 4.4]
.

Definition 6.3
.

We define the
vertex term

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=\operatorname{tr}_{R\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{\alpha}}(I_{\alpha},I_{\alpha})_{0}[1]}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\frac{\mathsf{A}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}}{1-t_{i}}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 4}\frac{\mathsf{A}_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{j}}}{(1-t_{i})(1-t_{j})},

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}

\displaystyle:=-Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}}+\frac{\overline{Z}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}}{t_{j}t_{k}t_{l}}-\frac{P_{jkl}}{t_{j}t_{k}t_{l}}Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}}\overline{Z}_{\alpha\beta_{i}},

\displaystyle\mathsf{A}_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{j}}

\displaystyle:=Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{j}}+\frac{\overline{Z}_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{j}}}{t_{k}t_{l}}-\frac{P_{kl}}{t_{k}t_{l}}Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{j}}\overline{Z}_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{j}},

and
\operatorname{tr}_{R\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{\alpha}}(I_{\alpha},I_{\alpha})_{0}[1]}
is given by (
37
).
Here
i,j,k,l\in\{1,2,3,4\}
are mutually distinct.
As before, the expressions in this definition should be expanded in ascending powers of
t_{i}
(though we will see shortly that
\mathsf{V}_{\alpha}
is a Laurent polynomial in the
t_{i}
).
When we want to stress the dependence of these expressions on
Z_{\alpha},Z_{\alpha\beta},Z_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
, we write
\mathsf{V}_{Z_{\alpha}},\mathsf{A}_{Z_{\alpha\beta}},\mathsf{A}_{Z_{\alpha\beta\gamma}}
. Referring to their corresponding ideals, we also denote these expressions by
\mathsf{V}_{I_{\alpha}},\mathsf{A}_{I_{\alpha\beta}},\mathsf{A}_{I_{\alpha\beta\gamma}}
respectively.

Recall that the normal bundle to
L_{\alpha\beta_{i}}\cong\mathbb{P}^{1}
is of the form (Section
5.1
)

\displaystyle N_{L_{\alpha\beta_{i}}/X}\cong\mathcal{O}(m_{\alpha\beta_{i}})\oplus\mathcal{O}(m_{\alpha\beta_{i}}^{\prime})\oplus\mathcal{O}(m_{\alpha\beta_{i}}^{\prime\prime}),

\displaystyle m_{\alpha\beta_{i}}+m^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}+m^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}=-2.

Definition 6.4
.

We define the
edge term

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}

\displaystyle:=\frac{t_{i}^{-1}\mathsf{B}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}(t_{j},t_{k},t_{l})}{1-t_{i}^{-1}}-\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}(t_{i}^{-m_{\alpha\beta_{i}}}t_{j},t_{i}^{-m_{\alpha\beta_{i}}^{\prime}}t_{k},t_{i}^{-m_{\alpha\beta_{i}}^{\prime\prime}}t_{l})}{1-t_{i}^{-1}},

where

\displaystyle\mathsf{B}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}

\displaystyle:=\mathsf{A}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}+\sum_{a\in\{j,k,l\}}\frac{\mathsf{A}_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{a}}}{1-t_{a}},

where
i,j,k,l\in\{1,2,3,4\}
are mutually distinct.
The expressions in this definition should be expanded in ascending powers of
t_{i}^{-1}
(!),
t_{j},t_{k},t_{l}
(though we will see shortly that
\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}
is a Laurent polynomial in
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4}
).
When we want to stress the dependence of these expressions on
Z_{\alpha},Z_{\alpha\beta}
, we write
\mathsf{E}_{Z_{\alpha\beta}},\mathsf{B}_{Z_{\alpha\beta}}
. Referring to their corresponding ideals, we also denote these expressions by
\mathsf{E}_{I_{\alpha\beta}},\mathsf{B}_{I_{\alpha\beta}}
respectively.

One can also consider the expressions of Definitions
6.3
,
6.4
for
Z\subset X
of dimension
\leq 1
. Then they reduce to the redistributions studied in
[
CK2
]
(in turn based on
[
MNOP
]
).

Definition 6.5
.

Fix
f\in F(X)
. Denote by
Z_{f}\subset X
the unique
T_{X}
-fixed 2-dimensional closed subscheme set-theoretically supported on
S_{f}
such that

Z_{f}\cap U_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}}=Z\cap U_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}}

for any
\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2},\alpha_{3}\in f
distinct. Then
Z_{f}
is determined by a single finite partition. Let
I_{f}:=I_{Z_{f}}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}
be the corresponding ideal, then we define the
face term
by

\mathsf{F}_{f}:=\operatorname{tr}_{R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I_{f},I_{f})_{0}[1]}.

Note that, since
Z_{f}
is proper,
\mathsf{F}_{f}
is a Laurent polynomial in the
t_{i}
.
When we want to stress the dependence of
\mathsf{F}_{f}
on
Z_{f}
we write
\mathsf{F}_{Z_{f}}
. Referring to the corresponding ideal, we also denote this expression by
\mathsf{F}_{I_{f}}
.

The main result of this section is the following:

Theorem 6.6
.

Let
X
be a toric Calabi-Yau 4-fold and
Z\subset X
a 2-dimensional
T_{X}
-fixed closed subscheme with proper support. Then

(39)

R\mathrm{Hom}(I_{Z},I_{Z})_{0}[1]=\sum_{\alpha\in V(X)}\mathsf{V}_{\alpha}+\sum_{\alpha\beta\in E(X)}\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta}+\sum_{f\in F(X)}\mathsf{F}_{f},

where
\mathsf{V}_{\alpha},\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta},\mathsf{F}_{f}
are Laurent polynomials.

Proof.

We first show the equality (
39
) and then establish polynomiality of
\mathsf{V}_{\alpha}
,
\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta}
,
\mathsf{F}_{f}
. Note that

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in V(X)}\operatorname{tr}_{R\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{\alpha}}(I_{\alpha},I_{\alpha})_{0}[1]}-\sum_{\alpha\beta\in E(X)}\operatorname{tr}_{R\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{\alpha\beta}}(I_{\alpha\beta},I_{\alpha\beta})_{0}[1]}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\alpha\in V(X)}\Bigg(-R\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{\alpha}}(I_{\alpha},I_{\alpha})_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\frac{\mathsf{A}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}}{1-t_{i}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\alpha\beta\in E(X)}\Bigg(\frac{t_{1}^{-1}\mathsf{A}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{2},t_{3},t_{4})}{1-t_{1}^{-1}}-\frac{\mathsf{A}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1}^{-m_{\alpha\beta}}t_{2},t_{1}^{-m^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}}t_{3},t_{1}^{-m^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha\beta}}t_{4})}{1-t_{1}^{-1}}\Bigg),

where the coordinates of summand
\alpha\beta
are labelled such that
L_{\alpha\beta}\cap U_{\alpha}=Z(x_{2},x_{3},x_{4})
and
L_{\alpha\beta}\cap U_{\beta}=Z(x_{2}^{\prime},x_{3}^{\prime},x_{4}^{\prime})
.

By Remark
6.1
, in order to calculate
R\mathrm{Hom}(I_{Z},I_{Z})_{0}[1]
, we are left with the contributions of (1)
\alpha<\beta\in V(X)
such that
\alpha\beta\notin E(X)
and
\alpha,\beta\in f
for some
f\in F(X)
; and (2)
\alpha_{1}<\cdots<\alpha_{N}\in V(X)
, with
N\geq 3
, such that
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{N}\in f
for some
f\in F(X)
. For any face
f\in F(X)
, we now label the vertices of
f
as
\alpha_{1}<\cdots<\alpha_{v_{f}}
, where
v_{f}=e(S_{f})
is the topological Euler characteristic of
S_{f}
. On
U_{\alpha_{i}}
, we use coordinates
(x_{1}^{(i)},x_{2}^{(i)},x_{3}^{(i)},x_{4}^{(i)})
such that
(t_{1}^{(i)},t_{2}^{(i)},t_{3}^{(i)},t_{4}^{(i)})\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}\cong T_{X}
acts by the standard torus action. We label the coordinates such that for any distinct
\alpha_{i},\alpha_{j},\alpha_{k}
, the first two coordinates of
U_{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k}}\cong(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{2}\times\mathbb{C}^{2}
are
x_{1}^{(i)},x_{2}^{(i)}
, then

\displaystyle\operatorname{tr}_{R\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k}}}(I_{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k}},I_{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{j}\alpha_{k}})_{0}[1]}

\displaystyle=\delta(t_{1}^{(i)})\delta(t_{2}^{(i)})\mathsf{A}_{f}(t_{3}^{(i)},t_{4}^{(i)})

\displaystyle=\delta(t_{1}^{(j)})\delta(t_{2}^{(j)})\mathsf{A}_{f}(t_{3}^{(j)},t_{4}^{(j)})

\displaystyle=\delta(t_{1}^{(k)})\delta(t_{2}^{(k)})\mathsf{A}_{f}(t_{3}^{(k)},t_{4}^{(k)}),

where

\mathsf{A}_{f}(t_{3},t_{4}):=Z_{\lambda_{f}}+\frac{\overline{Z}_{\lambda_{f}}}{t_{3}t_{4}}-\frac{P_{34}}{t_{3}t_{4}}Z_{\lambda_{f}}\overline{Z}_{\lambda_{f}}\in\mathbb{Z}[t_{3}^{\pm 1},t_{4}^{\pm 1}]

and
\lambda_{f}
is the finite partition determining the scheme
Z_{f}
in Definition
6.5
and
Z_{\lambda_{f}}:=\sum_{(i,j)\in\lambda_{f}}t_{3}^{i}t_{4}^{j}
. More generally, for any
\alpha_{i_{1}}<\cdots<\alpha_{i_{l}}
with
l\geq 2
, where for
l=2
we assume
\alpha_{i_{1}},\alpha_{i_{2}}
are not adjacent, we have

\operatorname{tr}_{R\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{\alpha_{i_{1}}\ldots\alpha_{i_{l}}}}(I_{\alpha_{i_{1}}\ldots\alpha_{i_{l}}},I_{\alpha_{i_{1}}\ldots\alpha_{i_{l}}})_{0}[1]}=\delta(t_{1}^{(1)})\delta(t_{2}^{(1)})\mathsf{A}_{f}(t_{3}^{(1)},t_{4}^{(1)}).

We define
N_{f}:=-(-v_{f}+\sum_{i=2}^{v_{f}}(-1)^{i}\binom{v_{f}}{i})=1
and

(40)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathsf{F}_{f}^{\prime}:=N_{f}\cdot\delta(t_{1}^{(1)})\delta(t_{2}^{(1)})\mathsf{A}_{f}(t_{3}^{(1)},t_{4}^{(1)})-\sum_{i}\frac{\mathsf{A}_{f}(t_{3}^{(i)},t_{4}^{(i)})}{(1-t_{1}^{(i)})(1-t_{2}^{(i)})}\\
&-\sum_{i}\Bigg(\frac{(t_{1}^{(i)})^{-1}\mathsf{A}_{f}(t_{3}^{(i)},t_{4}^{(i)})}{(1-(t_{1}^{(i)})^{-1})(1-t_{2}^{(i)})}-\frac{\mathsf{A}_{f}((t_{1}^{(i)})^{-m_{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i+1}}^{\prime}}t_{3}^{(i)},(t_{1}^{(i)})^{-m_{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i+1}}^{\prime\prime}}t^{(i)}_{4})}{(1-(t_{1}^{(i)})^{-1})(1-(t_{1}^{(i)})^{-m_{\alpha_{i}\alpha_{i+1}}}t_{2}^{(i)})}\Bigg).\end{split}

Then, by Definitions
6.3
and
6.4
, we have

\displaystyle R\mathrm{Hom}(I_{Z},I_{Z})_{0}[1]=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in V(X)}\Bigg(-R\mathrm{Hom}_{U_{\alpha}}(I_{\alpha},I_{\alpha})_{0}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\frac{\mathsf{A}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}}{1-t_{i}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\sum_{\alpha\beta\in E(X)}\Bigg(\frac{t_{1}^{-1}\mathsf{A}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{2},t_{3},t_{4})}{1-t_{1}^{-1}}-\frac{\mathsf{A}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1}^{-m_{\alpha\beta}}t_{2},t_{1}^{-m^{\prime}_{\alpha\beta}}t_{3},t_{1}^{-m^{\prime\prime}_{\alpha\beta}}t_{4})}{1-t_{1}^{-1}}\Bigg)

\displaystyle+\sum_{f\in F(X)}N_{f}\cdot\delta(t_{1}^{(1)})\delta(t_{2}^{(1)})\mathsf{A}_{f}(t_{3}^{(1)},t_{4}^{(1)})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in V(X)}\mathsf{V}_{\alpha}+\sum_{\alpha\beta\in E(X)}\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta}+\sum_{f\in F(X)}\mathsf{F}_{f}^{\prime}.

In order to complete the proof, we need to show
\mathsf{F}_{f}^{\prime}=\mathsf{F}_{f}
(Definition
6.5
), and
\mathsf{V}_{\alpha},\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta},\mathsf{F}_{f}
are Laurent polynomials.

Consider a vertex
\alpha\in V(X)
with neighbouring vertices
\beta_{1},\beta_{2},\beta_{3},\beta_{4}\in V(X)
(possibly fewer if
\alpha
lies on a non-compact edge). Recall that
Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{j}}
are Laurent polynomials. Consider the Laurent
polynomials

W_{\alpha\beta_{i}}
,
W_{\alpha}
of Definition
6.2
, which satisfy

(41)

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{\alpha}&=\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 4}\frac{Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{j}}}{(1-t_{i})(1-t_{j})}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\frac{W_{\alpha\beta_{i}}}{1-t_{i}}+W_{\alpha},\\
Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}}&=\sum_{a\in\{j,k,l\}}\frac{Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{a}}}{1-t_{a}}+W_{\alpha\beta_{i}}.\end{split}

Substituting into
\mathsf{V}_{\alpha}
gives an expression in terms of
W_{\alpha\beta_{i}}
,
W_{\alpha}
(and their duals). An explicit (lengthy) calculation shows that all denominators cancel and
\mathsf{V}_{\alpha}
is a Laurent polynomial.
Similarly, substituting (
41
) into
\mathsf{B}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}
, all denominators cancel and we find that
\mathsf{B}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}
is a Laurent polynomial. Since the numerator and denominator of

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}=\frac{t_{i}^{-1}\mathsf{B}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}(t_{j},t_{k},t_{l})}{1-t_{i}^{-1}}-\frac{\mathsf{B}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}(t_{j}t_{i}^{-m_{\alpha\beta_{i}}},t_{k}t_{i}^{-m_{\alpha\beta_{i}}^{\prime}},t_{l}t_{i}^{-m_{\alpha\beta_{i}}^{\prime\prime}})}{1-t_{i}^{-1}}

are zero for
t_{i}^{-1}=1
, we deduce that
\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}
is a Laurent polynomial in
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3},t_{4}
as well.

Recall the definition of
Z_{f}\subset X
of Definition
6.5
, and let
Z_{f,\alpha}=Z_{f}\cap U_{\alpha}
and
Z_{f,\alpha\beta}=Z_{f}\cap U_{\alpha\beta}
. Then

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{Z_{f}}:=R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I_{Z_{f}},I_{Z_{f}})_{0}[1]=\sum_{\alpha\in V(X)}\mathsf{V}_{Z_{f,\alpha}}+\sum_{\alpha\beta\in E(X)}\mathsf{E}_{Z_{f,\alpha\beta}}+\mathsf{F}_{Z_{f}}^{\prime}.

It is easy to calculate
\mathsf{V}_{Z_{f,\alpha}}=\mathsf{E}_{Z_{f,\alpha\beta}}=0
for all
\alpha\in V(X)
and
\alpha\beta\in E(X)
. Hence
\mathsf{F}_{Z_{f}}^{\prime}=\mathsf{F}_{Z_{f}}=R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I_{Z_{f}},I_{Z_{f}})_{0}[1]
and it is a Laurent polynomial because
Z_{f}\subset X
is proper.
∎

Remark 6.7
.

The proof of Theorem
6.6
leads to a nice expression for
\mathsf{F}_{f}
. For a
T_{X}
-fixed 2-dimensional subscheme
Z\subset X
with proper support, denote by
\lambda_{f}
the finite partition corresponding to the subscheme
Z_{f}
from Definition
6.5
. Let

\mathsf{A}_{f}(t_{3},t_{4}):=Z_{\lambda_{f}}+\frac{\overline{Z}_{\lambda_{f}}}{t_{3}t_{4}}-\frac{P_{34}}{t_{3}t_{4}}Z_{\lambda_{f}}\overline{Z}_{\lambda_{f}}\in\mathbb{Z}[t_{3}^{\pm 1},t_{4}^{\pm 1}],

where
Z_{\lambda_{f}}:=\sum_{(i,j)\in\lambda_{f}}t_{3}^{i}t_{4}^{j}
. Denote by
\alpha_{1}<\cdots<\alpha_{e(S_{f})}
the vertices of
f
, where
e(S_{f})
denotes the topological Euler characteristic of
S_{f}
. As before, for any
\alpha_{i}\in f
, we use coordinates
(x_{1}^{(i)},x_{2}^{(i)},x_{3}^{(i)},x_{4}^{(i)})
on
U_{\alpha_{i}}
such that
(t_{1}^{(i)},t_{2}^{(i)},t_{3}^{(i)},t_{4}^{(i)})\in(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}\cong T_{X}
acts by the standard torus action. Then

(42)

\mathsf{F}_{f}=\sum_{i=1}^{e(S_{f})}\frac{\mathsf{A}_{f}(t_{3}^{(i)},t_{4}^{(i)})}{(1-t_{1}^{(i)})(1-t_{2}^{(i)})}.

This equality should be interpreted carefully and can be proved as follows.
24
24

24

We thank Schmiermann for pointing out the correct interpretation of (
42
).
Firstly, writing
t_{1}:=t_{1}^{(1)}
,
t_{2}:=t_{2}^{(1)}
, we view each
t_{1}^{(i)}
,
t_{2}^{(i)}
as a function of
t_{1},t_{2}
determined by the coordinate change between
U_{\alpha_{1}}
and
U_{\alpha_{i}}
(
26
). Secondly, the left hand side of (
42
) is the expression in (
40
), which lives in
\mathbb{Q}[t_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,t_{4}^{\pm 1}]\subset\mathbb{Q}(\!(t_{1},t_{1}^{-1},\ldots,t_{4},t_{4}^{-1})\!)
, whereas the right hand side of (
42
) (a priori) is an element of
\mathbb{Q}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{4})
. We consider the field extensions

\mathbb{Q}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{4})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Q}(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})(\!(t_{4})\!)\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow\mathbb{Q}(\!(t_{1})\!)\cdots(\!(t_{4})\!),

obtained by expanding denominators in ascending powers of the
t_{i}
(e.g. for the first extension we expand
1/(1-f(t_{4}))=1+f(t_{4})+f(t_{4})^{2}+\ldots
for any
f(t_{4})\in t_{4}\mathbb{Q}(t_{1},t_{2},t_{3})[t_{4}]
).
Note that
\mathbb{Q}(\!(t_{1})\!)\cdots(\!(t_{4})\!)\subset\mathbb{Q}(\!(t_{1},t_{1}^{-1},\ldots,t_{4},t_{4}^{-1})\!)
. This embeds the right hand side of (
42
) into
\mathbb{Q}(\!(t_{1},t_{1}^{-1},\ldots,t_{4},t_{4}^{-1})\!)
and after multiplying left and right hand side of (
42
) by
\prod_{i}(1-t_{1}^{(i)})(1-t_{2}^{(i)})
, and using
\delta(t_{a}^{(i)})(1-t_{a}^{(i)})=0
, they are obviously equal.
25
25

25

Although
\mathbb{Q}(\!(t_{1},t_{1}^{-1},\ldots,t_{4},t_{4}^{-1})\!)
is not a ring, it is a
\mathbb{Q}[t_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,t_{4}^{\pm 1}]
-module.
This shows that the right hand side of (
42
) is in fact an element
\mathbb{Q}[t_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,t_{4}^{\pm 1}]\subset\mathbb{Q}(t_{1},\ldots,t_{4})
and it is equal to the left hand side of (
42
).

We use the following notation, borrowed from Nekrasov-Piazzalunga
[
NP1
]
, for the symmetrized
T
-equivariant
K
-theoretic Euler class. As before,
T\leq T_{X}
denotes the Calabi-Yau torus (
24
).

Definition 6.8
.

Consider the action of
T\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
on
\mathrm{pt}
, where
T=Z(t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}t_{4}-1)\leq T_{X}
as before, and we denote the equivariant parameter of the second torus by
y
. We define

[E]:=\widehat{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}E^{\vee},

for any class
E\in K_{0}^{T\times\mathbb{C}^{*}}(\mathrm{pt},\mathbb{Z}[\tfrac{1}{2}])\cong\mathbb{Z}[t_{1}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},t_{2}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},t_{3}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},t_{4}^{\pm\frac{1}{2}},y^{\pm\frac{1}{2}}]/(t_{1}t_{2}t_{3}t_{4}-1)
. In particular

[L]=L^{\frac{1}{2}}-L^{-\frac{1}{2}},

for the class of a
T\times\mathbb{C}^{*}
-equivariant line bundle
L
.

Next, we incorporate the
K
-theoretic insertion (Sections
3.2
,
3.4
)

\widehat{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(R\pi_{\curly P*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes y^{-1})

into the vertex formalism. At a fixed point
Z\in\curly P_{v}^{(-1)}(X)^{T}
, we have

\widehat{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(R\pi_{\curly P*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes y^{-1})|_{Z}=\widehat{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(R\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{Z})\otimes y^{-1}).

Using Čech cohomology, we deduce

\displaystyle R\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{Z})=

\displaystyle\sum_{\alpha\in V(X)}\Gamma(U_{\alpha},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}})-\sum_{\alpha<\beta\in V(X)}\Gamma(U_{\alpha\beta},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha\beta}})

\displaystyle+\sum_{\alpha<\beta<\gamma\in V(X)}\Gamma(U_{\alpha\beta\gamma},\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha\beta\gamma}})-\cdots.

These terms can be redistributed similar to Definitions
6.3
,
6.4
,
6.5

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{V}}_{\alpha}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{V}_{\alpha}-y\overline{Z}_{\alpha}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\frac{y\overline{Z}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}}{1-t_{i}^{-1}}-\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 4}\frac{y\overline{Z}_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{j}}}{(1-t_{i}^{-1})(1-t_{j}^{-1})}

\displaystyle\quad\ -y^{-1}Z_{\alpha}+\sum_{i=1}^{4}\frac{y^{-1}Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}}}{1-t_{i}}-\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq 4}\frac{y^{-1}Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{j}}}{(1-t_{i})(1-t_{j})}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{E}}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}+\frac{t_{i}}{1-t_{i}}\Bigg\{y\overline{Z}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}-\sum_{a\in\{j,k,l\}}\frac{y\overline{Z}_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{a}}}{1-t_{a}^{-1}}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\frac{1}{1-t_{i}}\Bigg\{y\overline{Z}_{\alpha\beta_{i}}-\sum_{a\in\{j,k,l\}}\frac{y\overline{Z}_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{a}}}{1-t_{a}^{-1}}\Bigg\}\Bigg|_{(t_{j}t_{i}^{-m_{\alpha\beta_{i}}},t_{k}t_{i}^{-m_{\alpha\beta_{i}}^{\prime}},t_{l}t_{i}^{-m_{\alpha\beta_{i}}^{\prime\prime}})}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\frac{t_{i}^{-1}}{1-t_{i}^{-1}}\Bigg\{y^{-1}Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}}-\sum_{a\in\{j,k,l\}}\frac{y^{-1}Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{a}}}{1-t_{a}}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad-\frac{1}{1-t_{i}^{-1}}\Bigg\{y^{-1}Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}}-\sum_{a\in\{j,k,l\}}\frac{y^{-1}Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{a}}}{1-t_{a}}\Bigg\}\Bigg|_{(t_{j}t_{i}^{-m_{\alpha\beta_{i}}},t_{k}t_{i}^{-m_{\alpha\beta_{i}}^{\prime}},t_{l}t_{i}^{-m_{\alpha\beta_{i}}^{\prime\prime}})}

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{f}:=

\displaystyle\mathsf{F}_{f}-yR\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{Z_{f}})^{\vee}-y^{-1}R\Gamma(X,\mathcal{O}_{Z_{f}}).

Remark 6.9
.

Using the notation of Remark
6.7
, we have

\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{f}=\mathsf{F}_{f}-\sum_{i=1}^{e(S_{f})}\frac{y\overline{Z}_{\lambda_{f}}(t_{3}^{(i)},t_{4}^{(i)})}{(1-(t_{1}^{(i)})^{-1})(1-(t_{2}^{(i)})^{-1})}-\sum_{i=1}^{e(S_{f})}\frac{y^{-1}Z_{\lambda_{f}}(t_{3}^{(i)},t_{4}^{(i)})}{(1-t_{1}^{(i)})(1-t_{2}^{(i)})},

where the two sums are elements of
\mathbb{Q}[t_{1}^{\pm 1},\ldots,t_{4}^{\pm 1},y^{\pm 1}]\subset\mathbb{Q}(t_{1},\cdots,t_{4})[y^{\pm 1}]
.

Recall that, in the toric case,
\mathrm{vd}\in\mathbb{Z}
(Lemma
2.11
) and we define invariants by the virtual localization formula (Definition
3.13
). Therefore, combining Proposition
2.13
and Theorems
6.6
,
5.29
(i), we obtain:

Proposition 6.10
.

Let
X
be a toric Calabi-Yau 4-fold and fix
v=(0,0,\gamma,\beta,n-\gamma\cdot\mathrm{td}_{2}(X))\in H_{c}^{*}(X,\mathbb{Q})
. Let
\curly P:=\curly P_{v}^{(-1)}(X)^{T}
and denote by
\mathrm{vd}
the virtual dimension and
\operatorname{rk}
the rank of the tautological complex. Then

\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}\rangle\!\rangle_{X,v}^{\mathrm{DT}}=\sum_{Z\in\curly P^{T}}\pm\sqrt{(-1)^{\mathrm{vd}-\operatorname{rk}}\Big[-\sum_{\alpha\in V(X)}\widetilde{\mathsf{V}}_{Z_{\alpha}}-\sum_{\alpha\beta\in E(X)}\widetilde{\mathsf{E}}_{Z_{\alpha\beta}}-\sum_{f\in F(X)}\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{Z_{f}}\Big]},

where the right hand side depends on a choice of
\pm\sqrt{\cdot}
for each fixed point.

Remark 6.11
.

For notational convenience, we considered the insertion

\widehat{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(R\pi_{\curly P*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes y^{-1}).

The insertion
\widehat{\Lambda}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}(R\pi_{\curly P*}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathcal{Z}}\otimes L)\otimes y^{-1})
, for an arbitrary
T_{X}
-equivariant line bundle
L
on
X
, can be easily incorporated as follows. Define
L_{\alpha}=L|_{U_{\alpha}}
,
L_{\alpha\beta}=L|_{U_{\alpha\beta}}
etc. Then, in the definition of
\mathsf{V}_{\alpha}
, we replace
y
by
L_{\alpha}^{*}y
and we denote the resulting expression by
\mathsf{V}_{\alpha}(L_{\alpha})
(and similarly for the edge and face).

We will now show that the vertex, edge, and face terms in Proposition
6.10
contain no positive
T
-fixed terms, which justifies writing
26
26

26

It follows from (
44
) in Section
6.3
that
\operatorname{rk}(\widetilde{\mathsf{V}}_{Z_{\alpha}})
is even. Similarly, it can be seen that
\operatorname{rk}(\widetilde{\mathsf{E}}_{Z_{\alpha\beta}})
is even. It is also clear from Definition
6.5
and Lemma
2.11
that
\operatorname{rk}(\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{Z_{f}})
is even.

\displaystyle\langle\!\langle\mathcal{O}_{X}\rangle\!\rangle_{X,v}^{\mathrm{DT}}=\sum_{Z\in\curly P^{T}}\pm

\displaystyle\prod_{\alpha\in V(X)}\sqrt{(-1)^{\frac{\operatorname{rk}(\widetilde{\mathsf{V}}_{Z_{\alpha}})}{2}}[-\widetilde{\mathsf{V}}_{Z_{\alpha}}]}\cdot\prod_{\alpha\beta\in E(X)}\sqrt{(-1)^{\frac{\operatorname{rk}\widetilde{\mathsf{E}}_{Z_{\alpha\beta}}}{2}}[-\widetilde{\mathsf{E}}_{Z_{\alpha\beta}}]}\cdot

\displaystyle\prod_{f\in F(X)}\sqrt{(-1)^{\frac{\operatorname{rk}\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{Z_{f}}}{2}}[-\widetilde{\mathsf{F}}_{Z_{f}}]}.

Lemma 6.12
.

\mathsf{V}_{\alpha}
,
\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta}
,
\mathsf{F}_{f}
have no positive
T
-fixed terms.

Proof.

For
\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta}
,
\mathsf{F}_{f}
we in fact show that they have no
T
-fixed terms at all. First note that
\mathsf{A}_{f}(t_{3}^{(i)},t_{4}^{(i)})
in (
42
) has no terms of the form
(t_{3}^{(i)}t_{4}^{(i)})^{n}
because tangent spaces to Hilbert schemes of points of
\mathbb{C}^{2}
at monomial ideals have no such terms (by a simple version of the argument with Haiman arrows in the proof of Theorem
5.29
(i)). From this it follows that
\mathsf{F}_{f}
has no
T
-fixed terms.

Next, we note that
\mathsf{B}_{\alpha\beta_{1}}
in Definition
6.4
, which only depends on variables
t_{2},t_{3},t_{4}
, is precisely
minus
the vertex term of Donaldson-Thomas theory on toric 3-folds
[
MNOP
, Sect. 4.9]
. This is known to have no terms of the form
(t_{2}t_{3}t_{4})^{n}

[
MNOP
, p. 1279]
. Therefore
\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta_{1}}
has no
T
-fixed terms.

Finally, we turn to the vertex term. We fix a
(\mathbb{C}^{*})^{4}
-invariant 2-dimensional subscheme
Z_{\alpha_{0}}\subset U_{\alpha_{0}}=\mathbb{C}^{4}
and we want to show that
\mathsf{V}_{Z_{\alpha_{0}}}
has no positive
T
-fixed term (we use a distinguished label
\alpha_{0}
). We pick a toric Calabi-Yau 4-fold
X
which is a partial toric compactification of
U_{\alpha_{0}}
in the following sense:
\alpha_{0}
has four neighbouring vertices
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{4}
and each
\alpha_{0},\beta_{i},\beta_{j}
, with
i\neq j
, are vertices of a
proper
smooth toric surface in
X
.
27
27

27

In fact, it suffices to add ten copies of
\mathbb{C}^{4}
in such a way that
X
contains precisely six
\mathbb{P}^{2}
’s which compactify the six coordinate 2-planes in
U_{\alpha_{0}}=\mathbb{C}^{4}
.
We consider the
minimal
proper
T_{X}
-fixed 2-dimensional subscheme
Z\subset X
such that
Z\cap U_{\alpha_{0}}=Z_{\alpha_{0}}
. It is then easy to see that for each
\alpha\neq\alpha_{0}
, we have
\mathsf{V}_{Z_{\alpha}}=0
. More precisely, in Definitions
6.2
,
6.3
: putting
Z_{\alpha\beta_{i}\beta_{j}}=0
for
(i,j)\neq(1,4),(2,4),(3,4)
,
W_{\alpha\beta_{i}}=0
for
i\neq 4
, and
W_{\alpha}=0
gives
\mathsf{V}_{Z_{\alpha}}=0
by explicit calculation. Therefore

(43)

R\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I_{Z},I_{Z})_{0}[1]=\mathsf{V}_{\alpha_{0}}+\sum_{\alpha\beta\in E(X)}\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta}+\sum_{f\in F(X)}\mathsf{F}_{f}.

The left hand side of (
43
) has no positive
T
-fixed terms since
\operatorname{Ext}_{X}^{3}(I_{Z},I_{Z})_{0}^{*}\cong\operatorname{Ext}_{X}^{1}(I_{Z},I_{Z})_{0}
and
\operatorname{Ext}_{X}^{1}(I_{Z},I_{Z})_{0}\cong\operatorname{Hom}_{X}(I_{Z},\mathcal{O}_{Z})
(
[
BKP
, Thm. 3.19]
), which has no
T
-fixed term by Theorem
5.29
(i). Since the edge and face terms have no
T
-fixed terms, the result follows.
∎

It is interesting to provide an explicit “half in
K_{0}^{T}(\mathrm{pt})
” of the vertex, edge, and face terms and hence the term under the square root symbol in Proposition
6.10
. Using the spectral construction, we give such expressions when
X=\mathrm{Tot}(K_{Y})
(Section
6.4
). In general, such an expression is exhibited in
[
NP2
]
. However, it should be noted that even after such a “halving”, one can still make any choice of signs assigned to the
T
-fixed points (Definition
3.13
). As we will see, in order to get interesting answers, one typically has to assign non-trivial signs even after “halving”.

Definition 6.13
.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mu}=\{\mu_{a}\}_{a=1}^{4}
be a collection of plane partitions corresponding to subschemes of dimension
\leq 1
of
\mathbb{C}^{3}
(and not all finite). We denote by
\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
the collection of solid partitions
\pi
such that
Z_{\pi}
has the property that its maximal subscheme without embedded points is
Z_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
(Definition
5.5
). We define the
(K
-theoretic)
\mathrm{DT}
topological vertex
by

\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{DT}}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}(q):=\sum_{\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\pm\sqrt{(-1)^{\frac{\operatorname{rk}(\widetilde{\mathsf{V}}_{Z_{\pi}})}{2}}[-\widetilde{\mathsf{V}}_{Z_{\pi}}]}\,q^{|\pi|},

where
|\pi|
denotes renormalized volume (Definition
6.2
) and
\pm\sqrt{\cdot}
is a choice of square root with sign for each
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
. In particular,
\mathsf{V}^{\mathrm{DT}}_{\mu_{1}\mu_{2}\mu_{3}\mu_{4}}(q)
depends on a choice of sign attached to each
\pi\in\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.

The previous definition extends the
\mathrm{DT}
topological vertex for curves studied in
[
CK2
,
CKM1
,
Mon
]
and for points in
[
NP1
]
. It also matches the DT topological vertex in
[
NP2
]

Remark 6.14
.

Let
I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}\in\curly P_{v}^{(0)}(X)^{T_{X}}

is a

-fixed proper 2-dimensional subscheme with no embedded 0-dimensional components.
Then

Let

\boldsymbol{\mu}

This definition is subject to the following restriction. We must assume that for all

, the vertex term
\mathsf{V}_{I_{B}^{{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}}}
has no positive

-fixed part (so we do not divide by zero). We expect that this is always the case when

is chosen such that

; or such that there are embedded curves in at most 2 directions and the underlying pure 2-dimensional subscheme is Cohen-Macaulay. Then the proof of Lemma

goes through.

28

The only modification is that this time the analog of the left hand side of (

) has no positive

(ii), (iii).

The previous definition extends the

Remark 6.15
.

Let
I^{\scriptscriptstyle\bullet}\in\curly P_{v}^{(1)}(X)^{T_{X}}
. Its scheme theoretic support
Z\subset X
is a
T_{X}
-fixed proper pure 2-dimensional subscheme.
Then
\mathsf{V}_{\alpha}
,
\mathsf{E}_{\alpha\beta}
,
\mathsf{F}_{f}
,
\widetilde{\mathsf{V}}_{\alpha}
,
\widetilde{\mathsf{E}}_{\alpha\beta}
,

can be determined in exactly the same way as in the
\mathrm{DT}
case with the following replacements. Instead of (
36
) we now take

Z_{\alpha}:=\operatorname{tr}_{\mathcal{O}_{Z_{\alpha}}}+\operatorname{tr}_{B_{\alpha}}

where
\operatorname{tr}_{B_{\alpha}}
is the
T
-representation of the
\mathrm{PT}_{1}
box configuration corresponding to

(Definition

). In contrast to the

case, the

-representation

Therefore it determines a box configuration

Recall that a box configuration

may also have moduli (labelled by

’s,
[
PT2
, Sect. 2.5]
).

and they are again Laurent polynomials.

29

29

]
